After 3 Marathons Without a Plan, I Finally Followed One. Here’s What Happened in NYC.

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Sports  |  Read Source →

My 3:45 goal didn’t go to plan, but the NYC Marathon still delivered a win.

The first time I entered the New York City Marathon drawing, I got in. I’m not sure what possessed me that day to apply, having never run another race in my life. I figured I’d never get in, so why not? I played soccer growing up, and when the world shut down during COVID-19, running became the easiest way to get out of the house and see my friends. That’s where my running journey began.

My NYC Marathon training also happened during my senior year of college, which brought its own challenges. While I was excited to run, I also didn’t want to miss out on any of the socializing that happened that year. I did my best to make it to every party, every club soccer game, and anything in between while running as much as possible. I was scared that running was going to infringe on my everyday life and I wouldn’t have time to keep having fun. Despite all of that, I had caught the marathon bug and after finishing the New York City Marathon in 2022, I went on to run the Paris and Chicago Marathons in 2024.

While training for those first few marathons, I didn’t have any real plan, except to not let it interfere with my social calendar. My strategy consisted of building up mileage in order to reach 20 miles about four weeks out from race day. In between, I pieced together training information from influencers on Instagram and Google searches (this was pre-ChatGPT!). Now that I’m a Runner’s World+ member, I have all those resources right at my fingertips.

Although I caught the marathon bug almost instantly, I also felt defeated after New York. I knew I could have done better. Between New York City in 2022 and Paris in 2024, I never ran more than 10 miles at a time, but I was running enough that I knew I could do it again if I really wanted. I followed a similar approach for Chicago in 2024: Build up to one long run about a month out, then fill the rest of the weeks with shorter runs and one medium-length run. It wasn’t much of a plan, but it worked for me. My times kept improving, and I broke four hours for the first time in Chicago.

Looking back, I realize that my no-plan approach was partly a way to protect myself. If I never said my goal out loud, I couldn’t feel disappointed if I missed it. Breaking four hours in Chicago changed that. For the first time, I wanted to see what I could do with a real plan.

I was now living in the city I had expected to call home ever since I left college, so I really wanted this race to feel meaningful. For the first time in my marathon career, I downloaded a Runner’s World training plan, the 16-week Break 3:45 plan to be exact, printed it out, and started tracking all of my runs. I wanted to beat my time in Chicago and get as close to 3 hours and 45 minutes as possible.

In the beginning, this felt super daunting. I had never done an interval run in my life nor had I ever intentionally sought out hills to make my runs feel harder than they already did. Nonetheless, I found my old Garmin Forerunner 55 and learned how to upload intervals to run up and down the West Side Highway. When the plan called for hills, I made my way back to the Queensboro Bridge for the first time since 2022, ready to face it with excitement instead of fear. Even while pretending to be excited, the incline is no joke!

For a while, I really felt like I had gotten the hang of things. I actually started to recognize what different paces felt like during the intervals, instead of sprinting and pulling back and looking at my watch while I ran. And, I could now confidently run over many of the bridges in New York City, notably the Williamsburg and Manhattan, even though they aren’t even part of the marathon course. I wasn’t following the plan exactly, but I was able to reorganize the days of the week to hit all the workouts.

Near the end of August (roughly week eight of the plan), it became hot all the time, I was constantly traveling for weekend trips, and I had started to lose the excitement of running. So while I was making sure to hit all of the long runs, the interval training fell off completely. I was a little disappointed in myself because the interval training runs were workouts that made me feel like I was getting stronger each week. I had never done this type of workout before, so I could tell I was getting better each time.

To find the joy of running again, I really started to lean on my friends. Every week, I’d send out a few texts to see who was available and wanted to go for a run with me. I ran the loop in Central Park almost every week and met up with my friends in Brooklyn to run every Friday morning. I started to explore parts of the city I had never been to before, which helped ground me in my training and remember that running is supposed to be fun and is an exciting way to explore the city.

From my previous experience, I knew that running the long runs was the most important thing for me to feel confident on race day. So much of marathon running is mental, so knowing that I could run 20 miles (and have run 26.2 before), was enough for me. So, while I started with a plan, I started reverting back to my tried-and-true method—the just make sure you run the long runs plan.

Outside of the training plan itself, I leaned on Runner’s World a lot. I didn’t have access to this in the past so I didn’t feel confident about planning for prerun meals or even how to take gels during the race. I also found that being able to read runner stories online made me feel more connected to the community, which helped me mentally with my training.

Plan or no plan, I’m proud of myself! My official time was 3:57:39, so while I did not achieve my goal of breaking 3:45, I did run a sub-4 race, and following the Runner’s World plan helped me get there. The last time I ran this race, I was a senior in college and thought I had it all figured out. I was going to move to New York City, get a real adult job, and start the life I had been imagining. Similar to my experience following the Runner’s World plan, this is not how everything went. Instead, I moved to a different city after graduating, eventually found my way back to New York, and my life is so much more full than I ever expected because of it. Maybe things aren’t always supposed to go to plan, and that’s a good thing!

11 Best Vacation Rentals in Riviera Maya for Beach Access, Private Pools, and Jungle Views

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: World  |  Read Source →

From the bustling city of Cancún to the somewhat more tranquil bohemian beach town of Tulum, the Riviera Maya is home to some of Mexico’s most idyllic beaches along the Caribbean. Featuring turquoise waters, windswept bays, and wide swaths of verdant jungle harboring hidden cenotes, this region of the Yucatán Peninsula delivers equal parts serenity and adventure. After living in Mexico for over 10 years, I’ve stayed in and driven through many of the towns and beaches that make up this region, and it's hard to choose a favorite.

As wonderful as the beachfront views, jungle tranquility, and local activities are here, the vacation rentals in the Riviera Maya are equally as impressive. They have a way of making the surrounding nature feel even more pristine. Whether you’re venturing to the region solo or traveling with friends and family, there are gorgeous options for nearly every type of traveler, each offering a personalized way to visit this corner of the Caribbean. Read on for my favorite vacation rentals in the Riviera Maya, from city escapes in Cancún to beachy stays in Tulum and everything in between.

We've selected these listings based on Superhost and Guest Favorite status, ratings, amenities, location, decor, editor stays, and previous guest reviews.

Number of guests: 2Bed and bath: 1 bedroom, 1 bathWhy we love it: Outdoor pool, Surrealist architecture, hammam, private hot tub

A true wellness escape, this one-bedroom loft inside the Babel Tulum community is perfect for travelers seeking a jungle refuge in one of Mexico’s most popular beach towns. The architecture alone is worth a stay: The community is an interpretation of the fantastical city and empire of Babylon, and this loft is one of many built around the communal outdoor pool and hammam. Inside, architectural details like chukum lime-finished walkways and a private hot tub on the terrace make this a cozy, romantic stay for two.

Location: TulumThings to do nearby: Sian Ka’an Biosphere Reserve, Tulum Beach Road, Gran Cenote

Surrounded by jungle on one side and beachfront on the other, this two-bedroom will enmesh you in nature—plus, it’s just steps away from some of the Riviera Maya’s best snorkeling. Located on the ground floor and in a tranquil gated community, this is indoor-outdoor living at its finest, with a swim-up pool that looks directly out to the Caribbean Sea. For travelers seeking a relaxing stay, this spot on a secluded bay feels worlds away from bustling Tulum Town.

The property includes an open-air observation tower, perfect for spotting toucans and flycatchers at sunrise, and offers close access to Sistema Sac Actun, the longest underwater cave system in the world.

The four-bedroom home (three king bedrooms and a room with two twins) was designed by the Mexico City–based architecture firm Productora and features open-concept living spaces, plus a spiral staircase that leads to a rooftop with a pool, outdoor seating, and expansive views of the jungle and the Caribbean Sea. You’ll have to draw straws for the primary bedroom: It features two private patios as well as its own hot tub and en suite bathroom.

Review: Park Hyatt Vienna, My Favorite Hotel In The Austrian Capital

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

The Park Hyatt Vienna is not only my favorite hotels in Vienna, but one of my favorite Hyatt properties in the world. With excellent service, nice amenities, delicious breakfast, and central location, our latest stay was another great reminder of what a superb property this is.

Although we could have connected directly to Zürich after landing in Vienna, I was able to force a 23.5-hour layover into the itinerary, which gave us a chance to stay to the Park Hyatt Vienna, a hotel I’ve enjoyed many times over the years but it was the first time my children had come along.

I booked this for 35,000 World of Hyatt points (versus a cash rate of over €700) and applied a “Guest of Honor” to the reservation, which essentially marks me as “Globalist” in the system.

Our Austrian flight landed at 11:30 am into VIE and I had emailed the hotel requesting an early check-in, specifically noting that we would be happy to forgo an upgrade in order to access our room a few hours early. Our request was honored.

The Park Hyatt Vienna is ideally located on Am Hof in the heart of the old town, an easy walk from St. Stephen’s Cathedral and many of the places I most enjoy visiting in Vienna.

Checking in, I was welcomed back to the hotel and the amenities were explained, including breakfast and pool hours (most important to my kids!).

We were assigned room 331, a standard room with two twins beds. While not an upgrade, the room was spacious enough and quite comfortable with a desk, round table, chair, and the two beds.

My daughter jumped right into bed to go to sleep (it was, after all, about 3:15 am in Los Angeles), but I pulled her out: no napping today would hopefully mean no jet lag tomorrow.

The bathroom featured really beautiful stonework behind the bathtub and included two sinks, a separate water closet, and walk-in shower.

The room had a wardrobe that included a coffee machine, hot water kettle, and mini-bar including snacks and drinks.

A welcome amenity was also waiting for us including various chocolates and candies.

The room had plenty of 220-volt outlets and also a safe to lock valuables. Unfortunately, the room did not have any USB-C ports or wireless chargers.

A primary reason I booked this hotel was for the pool and wellness. My kids love to swim and I can’t think of a better hotel pool than this one: we spent a lot of time here in the afternoon and evening.

The entire pool and spa area is posh, with a full-range of treatments available but nice say chairs with fresh peaches and plums, a locker room, and a wellness area with a sauna and steam room available to all guests.

Side note: the hotel has a cryogenic machine in the spa that will let you stand in -110ºC temperatures for three minutes…I was tempted and probably would have tried it had I been alone.

Thankfully, there was no drama this time in the sauna!

> Read More: Accused Of Being A Child Predator at Park Hyatt Vienna!

The fitness center is located in the spa area on the lower level, but accessible 24/7 with your room card and includes LifeFitness equipment. I awoke an hour before the kids and went down to workout…a nice way to start the day and fight jet lag.

You mustn’t skip breakfast here, for it is truly a treat.

The Bank is the most beautiful part of the hotel: an ornate dining room that was once the grand lobby when this hotel was still a bank. Breakfast is served from 7:00 am each morning until 10:30 am (11:00 am on weekends) and includes a delicious buffet plus an additional a la carte menu. If breakfast isn’t clouded in your rate, it is €58 each, which while steep is really worthwhile considering all you get with it.

There’s a bar section in The Bank that includes alcohol, including a large cocktail menu, and lighter food. It’s open from 9:00 am to 1:00 am on Monday – Wednesday, till 2:00 am on Thursday – Saturday, and from 5:00 pm to 1:00 pm on Sunday (good luck finding anything open on Sunday in Vienna…).

The hotel’s Viennese café includes cakes, coffee and chocolates, as well as alcoholic drinks and snacks, with an outdoor terrace. Open each day from 9:00 am to 8:00 pm.

Open from 9:00 am to 6:00 pm daily, the Lounge offers a more informal dining experience where you can enjoy hot and cold drinks plus a variety of food. Menu here.

Finally, 24/7 room service is available. Menu here.

There used to be really posh cigar lounge in this hotel, but now it is a beauty salon (Vienna has banned indoor smoking after being a smoker’s haven for so many years).

Our entire say was marked by one pleasant service encounter after another. I had a nice extended conversation with teh doorman and another great conversation with the agent who checked me in. We had a nice chat with our waitress in The Bank and the spa staff was also very friendly. I do speak German, albeit with an American accent, and appreciated that the staff was willing to engage in my less-than-perfect German as opposed to switching to English.

This was another wonderful stay at the Park Hyatt Vienna and I’ll sum it up by saying simply that I cannot wait to return.

My original review is below, dating all the way back to August 2014. Heidi and I went for my birthday just weeks after we got married…the hotel had recently opened and we had a great stay. It’s nice to see this hotel age so gracefully and what a joy it is to share this property and the lovely city of Vienna with my children.

Vienna is a stately city rich with majestic architecture, an erudite culture, and a regal sense of style. In the center of the old city, replete with cobblestone walkways, horse drawn carriages, and glistening statues of historical luminaries, a new Park Hyatt hotel has opened on the site of a former monarchical bank. My wife and I celebrated my birthday in Vienna last weekend and stayed for two nights at Park Hyatt’s newest property.

A historic property located on Am Hof 2 in the heart of the First District of Vienna, a UNESCO World Heritage site, the hotel’s location cannot be beat. Within a few paces is the Goldenes Quartier shopping district, St. Stephan’s Cathedral, Hofburg Palace, Spanish Riding School, and many museums.

We began the day in Frankfurt with an early flight on Austrian Airlines to Vienna. I sent a note to the main hotel e-mail, park.vienna@hyatt.com, just before takeoff requesting an early check-in and had a response waiting when I landed in Vienna – the room was ready.

From Vienna Airport you have two public transportation options for getting into the city – the City Airport Train (CAT) which travels non-stop to the heart of Vienna or a suburban train (S-Bahn) which calls at several stations on the way into town, taking about 15 minutes longer than the direct service. The CAT is 14EUR one-way while the S-Bahn is 4.40EUR. We took the S-Bahn into town, then connected on the underground train (U-Bahn) to Herrengasse Station, which is just a short walk from the hotel.

A smiling porter greeted us outside the door and took our luggage. Inside, a friendly young man checked us in, speaking first in English when he noticed my American passport but then switching to German upon the request of my wife that I practice my Deutsch! He escorted us upstairs and demonstrated the hi-tech features of our room. Like many Park Hyatt properties, lighting and draperies are electronically controlled by a central push-button unit.

I loved the room – a spacious standard room with tremendous natural light, a great view, and functionality. The room featured copious electrical outlets – an essential amenity when taking my business on the road, as I did here even on my birthday weekend. The layout of the couch in front of the bed in front of the desk nicely utilized the space in the room and the separate entry hall insulated the room from outside noise.

Two doors decorated in geometric art-deco style, closed off the bathroom and entry hall from the bedroom. The bathroom was very large (and well-placed mirrors made it appear even larger) and featured a sizeable bathtub, walk-in rain shower, two sinks, and a separate water closet.

A minibar featured an Illy coffee machine with several packets of complimentary espresso and snack and drink for purchase. The refrigerator did make a rather loud humming sound that could be heard even when the bedroom door was closed. I am a heavy sleeper, but my wife woke me up one night to “turn the climate off” yet it was not the air conditioning, it was the refrigerator! It was not terrible, just noticeable.

It was warm outside and we had a problem getting the air conditioning to activate. Turns out one of the windows was open just ajar – just make sure all your windows are tightly shut and your room key is in the slot by the door or else your air conditioning will not function. Once on, the room cooled down rapidly. Vienna boasts exceptional weather so most of the year there is no need for a/c anyway.

The hotel offers three dining options in addition to 24-hour room service; The Bank, Pearl, and The Lobby Lounge. Pearl bar features an outdoor terrace in addition to a contemporary indoor seating area and specializes in cocktails. A tapas menu is available with food as substantial as Wiener Schnitzel also available.

The Lobby Lounge offers drinks and light meals and sits next to the Living Room, a cigar and whiskey lounge that is one of the hotel’s best features. Admittedly, I see little value in setting fire to and then inhaling a plant that is linked to lung cancer but many of my friends and my brother hold a different opinion, so I find myself in cigar bars quite often. If you are a conneousier of cigars or just enjoy an opulent room to enjoy a drink in with friends, this bar is not to be missed. With plush couches and rich wooden paneling on the walls, I felt like I was part of the gentry mulling over world affairs in the cigar and brandy room onboard the Titanic.

Finally, The Bank is the hotel’s most opulent restaurant and central to the character of the property. Here, on the site of the former cashier’s hall, we had breakfast each morning. Breakfast was 33EUR and the buffet was expansive with all that you would expect in a comprehensive breakfast—fresh squeezed juices, cereals, fruits, breads, cold-cuts, cheeses, breakfast meats, and eggs. Coffee (it was tremendous) as well as pancakes, waffles, French Toast, and eggs are included in the buffet and can be ordered from your server.

We spent hours in here each morning enjoying a leisurely breakfast (breakfast ends at 10:30a during the week and 11:00a on weekends) and found the service to be warm, personal, and punctual. I thought everything was great – my wife had a problem with her eggs on the first morning (soft boiled eggs came out hard-boiled, then an omelet ordered with no cheese was delivered with cheese) but the issues were quickly remedied and did not reoccur the next morning. The juices were remarkably tasty (apple, orange, and even grapefruit were exceptional) and my favorite part of the breakfast was the cheese-covered cooked tomatoes.

Lunch and dinner are also offered each night in The Bank and you will find no menu posted online because the menu, sourced with local ingredients, changes often. Prices were moderate and I was prepared to dine at the restaurant for my birthday, but when mentioning that I was especially looking for fish, the concierge directed me to a restaurant called Kornat about 15 minutes away by foot, which specializes in fish and seafood.

So I missed trying out The Bank for an evening meal, but I did appreciate the restaurant recommendation for the food was amazing and we enjoyed a prime outdoor table thanks to a call ahead from the concierge.

I do have one gripe and only one true gripe about the property: the in-room wireless internet speed was unreliable. The Park Hyatt Vienna offers complimentary wired and wi-fi internet, which is a nice feature, but I found myself on multiple occasions banging my hand on the desk with disgust as poor connectivity led to unacceptably long page load times and the inability to make internet-based telephone calls. Other times, though, the interent worked perfectly well with pages loading fast and no issue with Skype calls or viewing YouTube videos. Admittedly, I did not not bring my ethernet cable converter for my MacBook Air so I was unable to test the wired internet speed.

A large fitness studio with state-of-the-art, brand new equipment was much appreciated. Several bikes, treadmills, and ellipticals filled one side of the room while strength training equipment and free weights filled out the other. The room itself, with brass candelabras and wooden floors was an elegant place to work out and apples, bottled water, refrigerated towels, newspapers, and magazines were available.

The gym overlooks the swimming pool below, which sits on the site of the former bank vault. With an ornate frieze relief and elongated white leather couches, it offers a refreshing antidote to the hot afternoon sun (or cold). Adjacent to the pool is a lounge with chairs to relax and also steam room and sauna facilities. My one regret is that we did not spend an extra day in Vienna just to lounge around the spa. Certainly, seeing Vienna is the priority if your time is limited, but this is a great complimentary benefit of the hotel that seems to go unutilized, as I saw many hotel guests and no one using the pool or spa lounge. Full spa services are available here in the Arany Spa and you can even see the original 3-ton bank vault door.

This hotel is a category six property, meaning it is 25,000 Hyatt Gold Passport points per night or 12,500 points plus a $150 co-pay (my preferred method of payment to help stretch out my points). The daily rate during the summer is about 375EUR or $500. Gold Passport availblity is good.

I can’t tell you how much I enjoyed the city of Vienna and both my wife (who is not usually a city person) and I wish to return soon. When we do, we will definitely choose the Park Hyatt again, allowing an extra day for the spa and enjoying at a more leisurely pace one of Europe’s greatest capital cities.

Tech company Huawei built its business on repeated crimes, prosecutors tell jurors

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Federal prosecutors urged jurors Monday to convict Chinese technology company Huawei, arguing that the company had grown by repeatedly committing crimes. (Photo from Shutterstock)

Federal prosecutors urged jurors Monday to convict Chinese technology company Huawei, arguing during closing statements in the ongoing 11-week trial involving trade secrets theft and sanctions evasion that the company had grown by repeatedly committing crimes.

According to a Monday story from Law.com, Meredith Arfa, the assistant U.S. attorney for the Eastern District of New York, told jurors that Huawei spent two decades pursuing growth “by any means possible.”

To illustrate the trade secrets allegations, Arfa played a video of a former Huawei employee removing part of Tappy, T-Mobile’s robot for testing phone touchscreens, from the carrier’s lab. She also showed side-by-side images of Cisco’s and Huawei’s router source code, saying the two matched exactly—down to an engineer’s stray comment mentioning someone named “Bob.”

“This simply couldn’t happen without copying,” Arfa said in her summation.

She further alleged that Huawei managers encouraged theft by paying bonuses for obtaining confidential competitor information, and that employees boasted about it on an internal forum.

On the sanctions compliance allegations, prosecutors said Huawei secretly controlled Skycom, a subsidiary that supplied sensitive technology to Iran’s government. Arfa said the company repeatedly lied about owning Skycom. She also pointed to admissions by Meng Wanzhou, Huawei’s chief financial officer, who signed a deferred prosecution agreement after spending three years under house arrest. Meng acknowledged that Skycom’s operations violated U.S. sanctions, and that she misled HSBC, Huawei’s primary bank, about its compliance, Arfa noted.

Huawei, one of the largest telecommunications companies in the world, denies the allegations, contending that the case rests on the independent actions of rogue employees who were disciplined.

The government announced the case in 2019. The company is represented by Jenner & Block, Sidley Austin and Steptoe.

There was gore in the original Resident Evil?

Most people don't associate the original Resident Evil with being overly grisly, but a lot's changed in the 30 years since it released. These days, we've become desensitized by dismemberment as a technical showcase rather than something to be horrified by, making 1996's Resident Evil look downright cozy next to today's horror pantheon. And yet, it was apparently shocking enough 30 years ago that series creator Shinji Mikami worried it wouldn't be fit for sale until Sony stepped in with some clutch packaging additions.

Mikami appeared on a Japanese podcast (timestamp) last month to talk about Resident Evil's legacy amid its 30th anniversary, but his comments haven't been widely reported until this week when Twitter user Genki started spotlighting the highlights. In one such example, Mikami tells the interviewers that OG Resident Evil was considered so "grotesque" that Capcom feared it wouldn't be fit for sale. We can only go off machine translation here as there's no English transcription, but Mikami says it was Sony that stepped in and saved the day by adding stickers to Resident Evil's packaging warning potential buyers of the game's gory nature.

Resident Evil creator Shinji Mikami says they were worried that the original Resident Evil game would not be allowed to go on sale because it was too grotesque but Sony had a solution that saved them. He said they didn't know what to do but Sony said that if they put a sticker on the game warning about the grotesque scenes then it would be okay to be released. Shinji Mikami says that really saved them. https://t.co/zgXIPPCIGQNovember 16, 2026

Mikami would continue to make excessively bloody games even beyond his time at Capcom with The Evil Within 1 and 2 at Tango Gameworks, but it's unclear if his next game has anything to do with the survival horror genre. He said in the same interview that his new studio Unbound is on track to release its debut title in 2027, but previous comments indicate he might be wanting to explore different genres after spending three decades scaring the bejesus out of players.

Does It Matter That We’ve Taken the “Mormon” Out of the Mormon Wives?

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

There is something about hearing “come ye saints, come ye sinners” that sets off a Pavlovian response in my brain; a physical trigger to sling the (dirty) Diet Coke back and turn the volume up. It means The Secret Lives of Mormon Wives is back, this time with its OC-set spinoff: The Secret Lives of Mormon Wives: Orange County.

In the show’s marketing, Hulu leans into the California setting as the clear divide between SLOMW: OC and its Utah predecessor. SLOMW: OC isn’t as ensconced in the shadow of the Mormon church as the Utah show is. Instead, it offers a more laid-back version of Mormonism (they drink! they get tattoos! they’re in throuples!). But in taking the “Mormon” out of the “Mormon Wives,” are we missing out on what makes the show so interesting?

It’s a bold choice to greenlight a spinoff about Mormons without actually casting any Mormons. In fact, of the eight new cast members (Jen Affleck is the only familiar face), only four identify as practicing Mormons at all: Mayci Neely’s identical-looking sister McCall DaPron, McCall’s bestie Jordan McClanahan, YouTuber Madison Bontempo, and Ashleigh Pease.

Meanwhile, the other half of the cast have either left the church altogether, as is the case with influencers Aspyn Ovard and Salome Andea, or were never religious to begin with like podcasters Avery Woods and Bobbi Althoff. It’s the “Molly Mormons,” or traditional Mormons, vs. the “Black Cats,” the rebels of the group. What connects these women is not so much a shared religion as a shared career path: influencing and podcasting.

SLOMW Utah’s strength was its portrayal of the contradictory rules of Mormonism. For example, what do you mean it goes against the church to drink caffeine but it’s totally fine to get high off laughing gas at a Botox appointment?? The rules only got more interesting as the show progressed and the women went from housewives to reality TV megastars overnight. Suddenly they were grappling with bigger religious conundrums: how to be both subordinate in the church and a breadwinner at home.

SLOMW: OC takes a different approach by focusing more on influencer mom drama, shifting away from the central tension of the show: women striving for independence and equality while navigating deeply patriarchal relationships. Take Mayci in season 5 when she fends off a fourth pregnancy request from her mother, sister, and husband (keep in mind, she just popped out baby #3!). Or Jessi, whose husband Jordan felt emasculated by the number of zeros in her paycheck.

In the Orange County-set spinoff, the men are curiously absent. Madison, Salome, and Aspyn are the only women to feature their romantic partners on screen—and even then it’s only a few moments of the show’s eight-episode run. The absence of the men feels like a strategic response to the DadTok hate that plagued the Utah show. But Hulu may have mistaken our TFP relationship fatigue for not wanting to see messy relationships at all.

Instead the show has channeled all that messy relationship tension into the women’s dynamics with each other. Their fights originate from the different ways they interpret feminism, religion, money, and power. Take McCall and Bobbi, for instance. McCall, who sees the group’s religious divide as her “McCalling” to convert, is the clear villain of season 1 because she tries to center Mormonism in the show. It’s Bobbi, hoodie pulled up and strings pulled tight, who brings McCall back down to earth and challenges her to see her faith through different perspectives.

“Show me the [bible] verse that says to be a bitch and talk shit on people, please,” Bobbi throws out in episode 4 after learning McCall made a dig about her comedy show and threatened to have another cast member replaced for not piling on Bobbi too. Later in the season, Bobbi confronts McCall over her drama with Aspyn Ovard and urges her to rethink her mean girl internet campaign. She points out that McCall might not agree with all of Aspyn’s life decisions, but Aspyn has a terminally ill child; McCall should be the bigger, more religious woman and show her some grace.

If the show wants to lean away from messy marriages, then it needs to lean into the messiness of religion in friendships. What are the boundaries that divide? What are the boundaries that unite? People hate McCall but, sorry, she is very good mess! Watching her try (and fail) to convert Bobbi into wearing pastels and saying a singular nice word about the church is the most fun I’ve had in a while. Without the religious tension, the show becomes hot, rich women fighting in public, and there are stronger contenders in that genre (hello, Real Housewives).

Maybe taking the “Mormon” out of “Mormon Wives” will rejuvenate a flailing brand, but the religious tension has to stay a priority regardless of the women’s religious affiliations. Taking the “Mormon” out of “Mormon Wives” is bad for the show—and bad for the mess!

Cursor Traces Malicious Claude Code Extension to Third-Party Breach

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

A compromised key issued to AI infrastructure provider Fireworks was used by threat actors to access Cursor data and credentials.

Cursor has traced the origins of a security incident involving a malicious version of Anthropic's Claude Code extension in its marketplace to a breach at AI infrastructure provider Fireworks.

As many as 5,000 users may have downloaded the tampered extension during the roughly 11 hours it was live on Oct. 29. It was a reminder of the risk organizations face when they rely on third-party marketplaces for software applications and extensions. It also illustrates how one security lapse can lead to a cascade of subsequent breaches and supply chain attacks.

When Cursor first disclosed the incident, the company said attackers had placed malicious versions of Anthropic's Claude Code extension in a cache serving its Visual Studio (VS) Code extension marketplace. Users who requested either of two specific cached versions of the extension (2.1.312 and 2.1.313) during the exposure window may have downloaded the malicious builds, the company said. The malicious extensions attempted to collect information about infected systems and establish persistent remote access, including by installing an attacker-controlled SSH key.

Related:Grok Bots Attempt to Access Corporate Data Without User Approval

Cursor quickly removed the malicious extensions, temporarily shut down its marketplace, and audited the store for signs of further tampering. Cursor also rotated its keys, rebuilt the marketplace, and worked with the cloud provider hosting the attacker's server to take it offline. The company's initial advisory, however, did not explain how the attacker might have gained access to its environment and planted the malicious extension in its extensions marketplace.

In an update on Nov. 13, Cursor filled in that blank, with a detailed description of what its investigation had unearthed in the days following the Oct. 29 incident.

The intrusion, according to Cursor, began when an attacker obtained an access key the company had issued to Fireworks, an AI startup based in San Mateo, Calif. Cursor said "the key was exposed by Fireworks," and the attacker used it to copy data from a read-only storage bucket containing historical coding-agent session records belonging to Cursor users. Most of the records were from 2025 and involved sessions that were not in Privacy Mode and that Cursor was permitted to use for model training purposes.

Some of the stored session records contained credentials that had not been properly redacted. The attacker used those credentials to access what Cursor described as select SaaS tools for accessing non-production source code repositories, customer support data, emails, usage metrics, and other data.

Related:F-Secure Identifies 26 Ways AI Can Harm Consumers, and Where to Stop Them

On Oct. 29, the attacker subsequently used one of the compromised credentials to tamper with the cache that Cursor uses to deliver extensions through its VS Code marketplace. The attacker inserted unauthorized copies of Anthropic's Claude Code extension in the marketplace where they remained available to users for about 11 hours.

"Anthropic did not publish this copy, and this was not a vulnerability in Anthropic's product," Cursor stressed in its update. "The marketplace source, Open VSX, never had the malware. At most 5,000 users who requested specific cached versions of the extension on October 29 potentially received the malicious build."

Cursor estimated that API credentials belonging to fewer than 0.01% of its customers were exposed in the incident. Historical coding-agent session records, or traces, belonging to fewer than 0.05% of users were accessed, the company said.

However, the supply chain attack goes back even further than Fireworks, which itself was a victim of a third-party breach. In a Nov. 9 update, Fireworks said a cloud provider hosting the AI company's R&D systems "experienced a security incident on Oct. 23." The attackers used the access to the R&D systems to obtain sensitive data, including API keys and access tokens.

Related:AI Makes Reverse Engineering Mainstream Software & Malware Far Easier

According to Fireworks, the available evidence linked the breach of its environment to a financially motivated cybercriminal group seeking to monetize unauthorized access to large language models. It's a practice known as LLMjacking, which Google and others have described as a rapidly emerging threat for organizations across multiple sectors in North America, Asia-Pacific, and other regions.

Following the incident, Cursor said it has implemented a broad security overhaul to reduce the risk of similar compromises. Immediate steps the company has taken include cutting off Firework's access to all its shared data sets, revoking and replacing potentially exposed credentials, and rotating internal credentials. The company has also tightened restrictions on how its systems and third-party service providers can communicate with one another and expanded automated scanning for secrets across its code repositories and internal data.

The Cursor incident is another indication of how the rapid adoption of AI-powered development tools and increasingly interconnected software ecosystems has exacerbated software supply chain risks. Recently, for instance, researchers uncovered more than 550 exposed secrets in Microsoft VS Code and Open VSX extension packages, including over 100 publishing tokens that could have allowed attackers to distribute malicious updates to legitimate extensions. Similarly, earlier this year researchers identified dozens of malicious GlassWorm VS Code extensions seeded in the Open VSX marketplace to steal developer credentials that attackers could use to spread poisoned software packages.

Illinois-based Jai Vijayan is a veteran, award-winning technology journalist with more than 25 years of experience covering cybersecurity. His information security reporting has explored everything from ransomware, nation-state threats, and identity security to AI risk, critical infrastructure protection, software supply chain security, cloud security and emerging enterprise technologies.

Over the course of his career, Jai has written news stories, feature articles, survey reports, white papers, and e-books for enterprise and technology audiences. He has also moderated panel discussions and executive roundtables featuring CISOs, security researchers, and industry leaders.

Jai previously served as senior editor at Computerworld, where he covered information security and data-privacy issues. His work has also appeared in CSO Online, InformationWeek, The Christian Science Monitor Passcode, The Economic Times, and other publications.

His work has earned multiple industry honors, including a Joint ASBPE Excellence Award for Best Coverage of Government IT, and a Joint Jesse H. Neal Award for wireless LAN security coverage. Jai holds a Master’s degree in statistics from Bangalore University, and studied broadcasting and electronic communication at Marquette University in Milwaukee.

EXCLUSIVE: Suzanne Lopez, who has been with American Idol producer Fremantle for nearly 23 years, is exiting the company.

Lopez has most recently been Chief Operating Officer for Fremantle’s North American operation, a role that she was promoted to in 2019. In that position, she oversaw legal and business affairs, HR, production, technology and security.

Before that, she was EVP, Business and Legal Affairs.

Jennifer Mullin, CEO, Fremantle, said, “Suzanne has made an exceptional contribution to Fremantle North America in her 22 years with the business. She has been integral to Fremantle’s success and growth, and we are grateful for her leadership, commitment, and the significant impact she has had.” Watch on Deadline

Lopez’s departure comes after former Endemol Shine North America chief Sharon Levy was hired as CEO, Fremantle Americas and Australia, reporting to Mullin. Levy, who previously ran the company behind Fox’s MasterChef and Fear Factor: House of Fear, will oversee shows such as American Idol and America’s Got Talent in her role running the company’s production operations across the United States, Mexico, and Australia. She starts in January.

Lopez has been with Fremantle since March 2004.

You Can Get This Micro-E-Reader for Under $60 Before Black Friday

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Black Friday is usually a great time to grab a new e-reader, but the ongoing RAM crisis has made that a question mark this year: Amazon recently raised the prices of all its Kindles by around $50 and refreshed the entire lineup, which means your odds of grabbing one at a big discount are low. But you aren't totally out of luck: If you haven't noticed, micro-e-readers—I'm talking credit card-sized, with screens that measure around four inches—are all the rage in 2026, and right now, you can pick up one of them for less than $60.

If you too can see the appeal of carrying all of your books with you on a device so small you'll forget it's even in your pocket, it's a great time to jump in: The recently released Xteink X4 Classic is currently 34% off in an early Black Friday sale, but you'll need to act quickly: The discount will soon be gone.

Xteink is a Chinese startup that shook up the e-reading world last year with the launch of the X4, a super bare-bones device that made up for its lack of features and usability with a cheap sub-$70 price point. Since then, a huge online community of fans has formed, sharing tips on Reddit and even coming together to crowdsource an alternate operating system that fixed a lot of early problems. Xteink quickly followed up the X4 with a slate of other devices, including the X3 and the X4 Pro, which adds a front light and touchscreen (at the cost of screen clarity and two physical buttons).

The X4 Classic was released in September, improving on the original X4 with quality-of-life upgrades like a larger battery, relocated page-turn buttons, a real-time clock, and a tilt-to-turn-page feature. It's also 6g lighter and a millimeter thinner than the X4, though there is a trade-off involved there: It drops the USB-C charging port in favor of magnetic pogo pins that require a separate adapter (included).

These upgrades also increased the cost: The X4 Classic typically retails for $89, $20 more than the discontinued X4 and only $10 less than the X4 Pro. But right now, the X4 Classic is $59, or around 34% off, and its lowest price ever. It's a good option for those who prioritize crisp, readable text above the conveniences of a built-in light or touchscreen, as those add extra layers between the inner screen and outer glass, and provided you understand what you're getting into: These devices support only e-books without Digital Rights Management (DRM), which means you can't use it to read your existing library of Kindle books. On the plus side, there's no longer a need to worry about flashing new firmware if you don't want to, as the native version has been totally overhauled and is now one of your best options.

In short, yes, Black Friday still offers discounts that can be rare throughout the rest of the year. If there’s something you want to buy, or you’re shopping for gifts, it’s a good time to look for discounts on what you need, especially tech sales, home improvement supplies, and fitness tech. Of course, if you need to save money, the best way to save is not to buy anything.

Follow our Black Friday coverage for the latest and best deals our team finds. Another good tip is to only buy what you need. It sounds simple, but making a list of the things you want before jumping into a sale with millions of products helps you focus and make smarter decisions.

No. If you find the product you're looking for with a major discount right now, get it. It's not likely to drop any lower, but it could always run out of stock. Take advantage of price matching or guarantees instead of waiting. Remember, you can always return products later if you change your mind. For the majority of products, the deal from Black Friday will be the same one on Cyber Monday.

Lucky Strike Entertainment CEO is proposing a take-private deal at about a 30% premium

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Free daily briefing on global business news.

Thomas F. Shannon offered $4.35 a share for all publicly held Class A stock, subject to board and special committee approval

Lucky Strike Entertainment CEO Thomas F. Shannon proposed taking the company private in a letter to its board, offering $4.35 a share for all publicly held Class A stock.

The price is an approximately 30% premium to Monday's closing price, Shannon said in the letter, which is dated Monday. Lucky Strike stock was up about 22% on Tuesday, and at its session high it had gained 35%, reaching $4.48.

Shannon said the proposal would need board approval and agreement on definitive transaction documents. He said he would not move forward unless a special committee of independent and disinterested directors approves it. The documents would also have to include a non-waivable condition requiring approval from holders of a majority of the Class A stock not owned by him or his affiliates.

Shannon said he would not need due diligence and that closing would not be subject to a financing contingency. He said he expects to consider letting preferred investors, and perhaps others who want to invest in a private business, take part in the resulting entity.

The letter describes the offer as an expression of interest that may be withdrawn or modified. No legal obligation would arise until the parties sign definitive documents. If the special committee or a majority of public holders rejects the deal, Shannon said, he intends to remain a long-term shareholder.

Shannon said the stock has generally traded down since an all-time high in 2023 and now trades at a fraction of the price at the company's 2021 de-SPAC listing. He attributed the decline mainly to "market conditions affecting current investors' attitude about site-based businesses generally." He said that has left the stock with very limited institutional and analyst support and a very limited public float.

Shannon said going private would let public shareholders receive a premium and would save the company the cost and distraction of staying public. Jones Day is advising him, and he said the special committee would retain its own legal and financial advisers.

Lucky Strike halted its dividend this month under a plan to pay down debt, and the stock fell to an all-time low as a result, according to Bloomberg. Its loan due in 2032 was put at roughly 80 cents on the dollar, according to data compiled by Bloomberg. That is up from a price under 75 cents at the end of October, which Bloomberg said was far beneath the level that usually marks distress.

S&P Global $SPGI Ratings downgraded the Virginia-based bowling and water park operator in September, moving its grade from B to B-, according to Bloomberg.

The Hidden Financial Stress of Thru-Hiking

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

For better or worse, I was born with the fiscal sensibilities of a Depression-era grandmother. This can be both an asset and a liability for thru-hiking. Back in the day, my frugal spending habits made it easier to save enough money to comfortably thru-hike the Appalachian Trail, but they were also an unexpected handicap once I was actually on trail.

People rarely talk about how psychologically challenging it can be to live with the stress of spending without earning for the six-ish months that a long thru-hike usually takes, and I definitely wasn’t prepared for it. Money stress comes in many forms, but most people experience it one way or another — and that struggle can follow you out onto the trail.

In today’s challenging economy, it’s more important than ever for aspiring thru-hikers to consider not just whether they can afford to hike, but how they’ll cope with the psychological stress of watching their savings dwindle with each passing mile. It’s easy to take fiscal responsibility too far and get caught up in an unhelpful scarcity mindset. Don’t let that be you.

Hiking is hard. Don’t make it harder by beating yourself up for literally no reason.

Thru-hikers often talk about how easy it is to spend way too much money on zero days. For me, spending money in town was one of the hardest things about the trail. I’m not saying I didn’t spend — the lure of a soft bed and a hiker-sized restaurant meal was usually enough to overpower my frugality — but I agonized over every purchase in every single town. There were times when I even felt so guilty about the spending that I considered ending my hike early.

It wasn’t just that I was spending money without replacing it; it was that I wasn’t doing anything about it. I wasn’t applying for jobs or upskilling or preparing myself to reenter the job market. I was just existing in the woods day in and day out, being thoroughly unproductive, with no particular plan for after the trail. This was apparently too much for my little capitalist brain to handle.

This mindset would have been more reasonable if lack of funds was a serious threat to my thru-hiking goals. But I had been fortunate enough to save enough money that I didn’t need to worry about running out mid-hike. I spent half my time worrying about running out of money for literally no reason.

Me mentally berating myself for overspending on name-brand peanut butter, probably.

What is the solution? Should I have saved even more money ahead of time? Of course that wouldn’t hurt, but such is the nature of anxiety that I doubt it would have stopped me from stressing. Should I have taken out a Coursera subscription and spent my evenings in camp learning how to code, or treated every impromptu hiker gathering like a networking event?

Obviously not. In reality, there’s usually no easy solution to the assorted neuroses that play out in our heads. In my case, it was just a matter of learning to live with the uncertainty of not having an income or a guaranteed path back into paid work after the hike. I was afraid of not having enough or of being unable to find another job, and I just had to face that fear.

The AT forced me to confront my own limiting beliefs around money. In the years since that first thru-hike, I’ve gotten a little more relaxed about spending money on things I know I can afford without feeling guilty about it. I haven’t abandoned my frugal tendencies, but they don’t need to dictate every decision I make. I credit the trail for that growth.

“I wonder if I can get another 200 miles out of these shoes before I replace them. I wonder if I should get on a cheaper insurance plan next year. Do I have enough money to buy name-brand peanut butter in the next town? Should I get a different credit card? Should I go stoveless to save on fuel? “

I’ve written a few articles over the years about my peculiar hiking-related neuroses. As always, I want to clarify in the midst of all this doom and gloom that thru-hiking was and is one of the great joys of my life, despite my assorted pathologies and the mistakes I’ve made along the way.

Every thru-hiker will face struggles both external and internal; learning to deal with and overcome them is part of the experience, and this is just one more example. As always, do everything you can to prepare for your thru-hike, accept that you can’t plan for everything, and then enjoy the journey, hardships and all.

Ripple’s RLUSD Supply Hits Record $2.7 Billion, With Ethereum Holding Majority Over XRP Ledger

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

RLUSD supply has climbed to $2.7 billion, indicating rising on-chain demand for its stablecoin, which operates mostly on Ethereum, according to data.

Ripple’s RLUSD stablecoin has achieved another milestone. Its total market supply has reached $2.7 billion, making a new high level in circulation, according to data reported on the X platform. RLUSD supply climbed to the $2.7 billion record on Sunday, Nov. 15, a 57% rise from the $1.72 billion mark seen on Sept. 14, data revealed.

The market supply of the Ripple-backed stablecoin has surged to a new ATH, exceeding the previous $1.72 billion milestone set in September. According to the data, RLUSD’s circulating supply currently stands at $2.7 billion, up from the $1.2 billion level reached in January. This rise signals an expansion of RLUSD’s liquidity across the wider blockchain ecosystem.

The supply expansion indicates rising demand for RLUSD as global customers gradually embrace the stablecoin for payments and DeFi applications. This surge positions RLUSD as a ‘digital dollar’ that allows users to execute cross-border payments and on-chain settlements.

The growth is pushing RLUSD higher into the stablecoin market. Now, the asset ranks as the 7th-largest stablecoin by market supply. The more supply means deeper liquidity across the public blockchain ecosystem, supporting cross-border payments and on-chain utility.

The Ripple-focused institutional stablecoin has reached another milestone, increasing its market supply to $2.7 billion. Interestingly, a large amount of this freshly issued liquidity operates on external public blockchains, not within the XRP Ledger network.

Metrics show that a large share of the new RLUSD supply sits on Ethereum, with Ethereum holding more than those in the XRP Ledger. This indicates rising use of Ethereum as a liquidity and settlement network for the stablecoin, while RLUSD continues to expand across protocols.

On-chain data indicates that out of the $2.7 billion in RLUSD circulation, about $1.73 billion runs on Ethereum. The XRP Ledger holds around $855 million, representing about 30% of the circulation.

Over time, Ethereum’s share of the supply has risen to about 60%. This shift is because Ethereum offers a deeper DeFi market, including lending and more. XRP Ledger is closer to activating its native lending infrastructure.

Joanna Gaines Shares a Sweet Moment at Her Family’s Waco Farmhouse as Her Youngest Helps Celebrate the Holiday Season a ‘Little Early’

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Joanna Gaines and her youngest child, Crew, are busy decking the halls of the family’s beautiful Waco, TX, farmhouse.

While some people are staunchly against putting up a Christmas tree before Thanksgiving, the Magnolia co-founder has already brought some seasonal joy into her home.

In a Nov. 15 Instagram Post, Joanna, 48, shared a video of her 8-year-old son excitedly turning on the family’s tree—a stunning display of twinkling lights and metallic-hued baubles.

The "Fixer Upper" star acknowledged the potentially premature timing of the decor with a tongue-in-cheek caption, writing, “For those who like celebrating the season a little early…”

The audio the mom of five selected for the clip really drove the message home, as she selected a song called “Christmas in November,” with lyrics that say, “They say it’s a little early, I say it’s right on time.”

This particular corner of the HGTV family’s dwelling was also featured on the cover of the Winter 2026 issue of “Magnolia,” with Joanna donning a festive red gown as she poses in front of a selection of jars of holiday candy.

The tree makes an appearance on the cover, as well, shining alongside the statement-making picture-frame windows and rustic checkerboard flooring that make the Gaines home so recognizable.

Set on 40 acres, the property has plenty of room for a large family to celebrate together. The family purchased the home back in 2012, and at the time, it was a quaint two-bedroom abode. After countless improvements—which most recently included an extensive kitchen renovation—it's become a home Joanna has come to love, despite being unsure of it initially.

Even though it’s only mid-November, the holiday season has been on Joanna’s mind for quite a while already as she plans ahead for all Magnolia has in store this winter. In fact, she kicked things off with a Sept. 9 post, when she first brought out a variety of wreaths and garlands for a holiday photoshoot.

In a Nov. 10 editor’s note in Magnolia Journal, she wrote about her philosophy as she heads into another merry time of year.

“Every season holds a blend of the big and small, the milestones and the everyday, the photo-worth celebrations and the not-so-picture-perfect moments,” she wrote. “There’s something about the holidays that bridges the gap between these.”

She noted that as the years go by, she’s “come to realize” that the most important memories are the ones that might not be captured with a camera, and exist only in memory.

“Maybe there’s no physical evidence they happened at all, except for the fact that you were there—and you felt it,” Gaines continued. “It’s these softer moments that give us life and ground us in presence. The days that held no agenda, the mornings I took slow, the smiles you won’t see in my camera roll because I was simply bearing witness.”

But that’s not to say she’s not planning make a splash when it comes to home decor.

In a chat with USA Today, Joanna revealed that her approach to holiday decorating has gotten more over-the-top as the years go by.

“The older I get, the more free I feel to deck the halls,” she said. “I do think this is the one time of year where you can really go big if you want to—or keep things timeless and simple.”

Although she’s well on the way to turning her family’s corner of Waco into a winter wonderland, Joanna understands the importance of staying true to one’s own desires.

“There’s no right or wrong because the holidays really are so personal and sentimental,” she said. “It’s just a matter of what you love and how you want your home to feel for your family.”

As excited as Joanna seems to be for the most wonderful time of the year, she doesn’t appear to be blowing past any holidays too quickly. She also has big plans for turkey day, an occasion that she looks forward to for its delicious meals.

“Food really is the center of our holidays,” she told USA Today, remarking that she’s been honing her own traditional recipes since she first married husband Chip Gaines back in 2003. “Thanksgiving especially is the day I can taste all year long because our family really does make the same dishes year after year.”

Meta is testing a new standalone messaging app called Direct

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: World  |  Read Source →

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Direct lets you access Instagram chats in a dedicated app, but it isn’t widely available yet.﻿

Direct lets you access Instagram chats in a dedicated app, but it isn’t widely available yet.﻿

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Meta announced Direct, a new messaging app from Instagram that it’s currently testing in Turkey and Thailand on iOS and Android. Direct is essentially Instagram’s direct messaging without Instagram’s other features. Meta says your Instagram chats will sync across both apps if you login with the same account. You can also sign up with an email or phone number, but Meta says if you use Direct without an Instagram account, “only your contacts can start a chat with you or add you to a group.”

This is far from Meta’s first messaging app — it also has Messenger, which requires a Facebook account to use, and WhatsApp, which Meta (then Facebook) acquired in 2014. Meta also recycled the “Direct” name, which it previously used for a Snapchat-like app it shut down several years ago.

The new Direct app has a UI similar to Instagram’s messaging tab, but with extra features for customizing chats and sending different types of media, including:

• Draw: Draw and sketch directly inside your chat threads.

• Notes: Catch quick updates from friends right at the top of your inbox.

• Stickers: Create custom stickers to express yourself and share with friends and family.

• Voice Effects: Add fun audio effects like crowd laughter or underwater sounds to your replies.

• Themes: Personalize your chat backgrounds with custom visual themes.

Meta says users have to be at least 13 years old to use Direct, and teens who log in with their Instagram account will have the same Teen Account messaging protections and restrictions on Direct.

Hayden Panettiere’s Mother Blames Brian Hickerson For Star’s Death

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

While the entertainment world continues to mourn the death of Nashville and Heroes star Hayden Panettiere, her mother, Lesley Vogel, is placing blame squarely on Panettiere’s former boyfriend, Brian Hickerson.

On Monday, Vogel gave her first public interview since Panettiere’s Aug. 16 death, speaking with Inside Edition about the often estranged relationship she had with her daughter and her emotions following her death. “Sadly, she allowed Brian to reel her back in,” she said. “Hayden had clearly let Brian somehow take over many aspects of her life.” (Hickerson has denied any invovlement with Panettiere’s death and has not been charged with a crime.)

Panettiere was found in her Greenville, South Carolina home, after police responded to calls for an unresponsive woman. She was 36 years old. In a public statement provided by her representatives, her father Skip Panettiere called her an “incredible light and a force of nature who brought immeasurable love and joy to all who knew her — and to the millions who watched her onscreen.” In Sept., the Greenville County Coroner’s Office revealed her cause of death as the “toxic effects” of fentanyl and other drugs. Tests found traces of Xanax, the muscle relaxant Methocarbamol, and Quetiapine, a drug that is prescribed for schizophrenia, bipolar disorder, or depression, but the coroner said there were no signs of trauma or foul play.  On Oct. 28, the Greenville Police Department closed their investigation, ruling Panettiere’s death as “accidental.”

Throughout her time in the spotlight, Panettiere was open about her struggles with mental health; including postpartum depression following the birth of her daughter but also anxiety and substance abuse issues tied to her early brushes with fame. She also had a publicly contentious relationship with Hickerson, who remained her on-again-off-again boyfriend until the time of her death. In 2020, she requested a restraining order against him. And in her May memoir, This Is Me: A Reckoning, Panettiere described their relationship being fraught with emotional abuse and physical violence. At the time of her death, Panettiere had recently finished a stint in a rehab facility. And according to a police report obtained by NBC News, Hickerson and his brother Zach were both in the home at the time of the actresses’ death. When news of Panettiere’s death was first released, Vogel told NBC News that she had been extremely concerned about her daughter’s relationship with Hickerson. “This person in her life that we have been trying to get rid of for quite some time was with her at her death, and that was Brian Hickerson,” she said at the time. “There is a lot of history in the past that Brian has enabled Hayden many times, and this is why her father and I had been concerned for a long time about him.”

But in her most recent interview with Inside Edition, Vogel placed the blame firmly on Hickerson. When she was asked if Hickerson was responsible for Panettiere’s death, she responded, “Absolutely. There’s no doubt in my mind. And I think that he knows that.”  Trending Stories Watch Buffalo Springfield Come Together for the First Time in Many Years Jennifer Lopez Teases New Music on ‘Fallon’: ‘This Does Feel Like a Happy Era’ Cynthia Erivo, Travis Barker Honor Phil Collins With Big Vocals, Big Drums at Rock Hall Ceremony Priscilla Presley Scores Partial Win Over Fraud Suit, But Claims About Lisa Marie’s Death Can Stand

Robert Pattinson’s James Bond Needs a Killer Cast. Here’s Who Should Join Him.

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

A great 007 movie isn’t just about the man himself. Now we can start to find the next Bond Girl, villain, and more—and really have some fun.

OUR LONG INTERNATIONAL nightmare is finally over: We have a new James Bond. On Monday, Robert Pattinson was announced as 007, beating out contenders that included Callum Turner, Jack Lowden, and Josh O’Connor. Considering Pattinson is currently double dipping as Bruce Wayne—and the fact that Bond is historically a star-making role, rather than a role for an established star—his casting won’t be everyone’s cup of tea. (My two cents: he would’ve been a god-tier Bond villain.)

Still, it’s hard to deny that Pattinson is going to crush the role. From buzzy indies like The Lighthouse and High Life to blockbusters like Mickey 17 and The Odyssey, he’s long been on a heater. (Nobody using Edward Cullen as a punchline against him is further proof of that.) For Amazon, whose stewardship of the Bond franchise was always going to be met with scrutiny, there’s no shame in making the safe, sensible choice.

But landing on a new 007 is only the beginning. James Bond isn’t just about the title character: The franchise can also be a career-defining move for actors enlisted to play recurring characters (M) and archetypes (the gimmicky henchmen) that bring the world to life. For Pattinson and director Denis Villeneuve, this new Bond entry needs to be populated with the very best supporting players Jeff Bezos’s money can buy.

With that in mind, here’s our dream casting for the seven most important non-Bond roles in the new film.

The Bond franchise has been around for decades, and the films are often a reflection of both the world around them and the current state of moviemaking. The Timothy Dalton era was gritty and grounded as the Cold War wound down; the Daniel Craig movies were serialized in the age of cinematic universes. For a modern-day Bond, then, we should have a villain that feels made for the 2020s. In other words: cast a sinister nerd—and more specifically, cast Samuel Blenkin.

In biopics and documentaries alike, tech bros are cinema’s latest villain du jour. Between the untold economic and environmental damage they've caused in the real world, nobody is going to feel bad about these guys being painted in an unflattering light. In that respect, Blenkin would be a fun casting. If Tenet was a great test run for Pattinson as 007, then Alien: Earth, in which Blenkin plays an impetuous tech bro with the gall to mess around with Xenomorphs (!!!), is proof that he could bring the same (respectfully) punchable energy to Bond. Say what you will about Zack Snyder’s DC movies, but picking the actor who played Mark Zuckerberg to be his Lex Luthor was ahead of the curve. Bond would be wise to meet the moment.

Runner-up: Jensen Huang (with the leather jacket, obviously).

The next M has big shoes to fill. Dame Judi Dench played the character across eight films and two separate Bonds over a period of 20 years, and Ralph Fiennes then made the role his own (as he always does), taking over the mantle for Daniel Craig’s final three films.

But sometimes, the obvious pick is the right one. Emma Thompson is acting royalty with a commanding air of authority, which is exactly what M calls for. I can just as easily picture her chastising Bond for a reckless bit of espionage as I can see her delivering some characteristically bone-dry British wit. Imagine how fucking electric a Pattinson-Thompson tête-à-tête would be.

Runner-up: Kristin Scott Thomas or Fiona Shaw.

We haven’t had an American Bond Girl since Halle Berry in Die Another Day, a film that, unfortunately, doesn’t hold up on rewatch on account of [gestures at Pierce Brosnan surfing a CGI tsunami]. Let’s have Bond dip back stateside with one of Hollywood’s most exciting young stars—and one whose name already sounds like that of a Bond Girl—in Chase Infiniti. Her resume is small but impressive: the emotional center of Paul Thomas Anderson’s Best Picture-winning One Battle After Another, a supporting role in Apple TV’s Presumed Innocent, and the lead of the Handmaid’s Tale spinoff The Testaments. And if Infiniti’s on-screen work isn’t enough to move the needle, she’s rocked enough red carpet looks to prove she knows how to make an eye-popping entrance. For a Bond Girl, that’s half the battle.

Runner-up: Margaret Qualley, mostly because she basically auditioned to be a Bond Girl at the 97th Academy Awards’ Bond tribute.

Aimee Lou Wood’s vibe as a performer is, in a word, cute. In her most notable performances—The White Lotus and Sex Education chief among them—there’s a natural warmth and mischievousness that would make her a fun scene partner for Pattinson’s Bond. Moneypenny is traditionally the one person at MI6 who can get flirty with Bond without worrying about getting a lecture from M after the fact. Wood has the charm and comic timing to make that dynamic sing.

Runner-up if Moneypenny becomes Irish: Nicola Coughlan.

Pattinson, Anne Hathaway, and (quite deservedly) Samantha Morton have been receiving much of the praise for their work in The Odyssey, but Himesh Patel’s beleaguered Eurylochus might be the film’s unsung hero. Now, he can pivot to playing the Bond franchise’s underappreciated MVP in Q: a perpetually put-upon tech genius who has to watch a reckless womanizer repeatedly destroy million-dollar prototypes. Patel has the dry, exasperated delivery down pat, and he’d be a perfect straight man for Pattinson’s particular brand of chaos (and it would mark a more-than-welcome Tenet reunion). Perhaps Q will belatedly give Patel the accolades he deserved for The Odyssey. (Also known as the Rhea Seehorn Principle.)

Runner-up: Matt Berry in character as Laszlo Cravensworth.

This might be recency bias, because I just watched Kelvin Harrison Jr. in The Hunger Games: Sunrise on the Reaping (say that three times fast). Harrison Jr. is Beetee Latier in the prequel, who was previously played by none other than … Jeffrey Wright. Credit where it’s due: Harrison Jr. does a mean Jeffrey Wright impression in the film.

However, we don’t need to see Harrison Jr. reheat his Wright impression in the new Bond. What I’ve admired in everything I’ve seen him in is how chameleonic he can be—to the extent that you could conceivably cast him in just about any role and he’d find a way to make it his own. (There’s no better evidence that he could play a killer spy than his performance in the psychological thriller Luce, where his character is eerily hard to get a read on.) I want a fresh take on Leiter, and the one thing we can say for Harrison Jr.: he’ll deliver on that promise.

Runner-up: John David Washington for peak Tenet vibes.

Look, I get it: Do we really need Tormund Giantsbane 2.0 in a Bond movie? But Hivju doesn’t deserve to be defined by that role for the rest of his career. He’s got the kind of outsized screen presence that demands an at-bat as a Bond henchman. (You can totally picture him getting excited by Bond drawing blood during a fight.) Sure, Villeneuve isn’t exactly known for humor in his projects, but Bond films have a long tradition of colorful henchmen, and Hivju could dial up the menace without taking himself too seriously. Give him a gimmick, a tweed suit, and no more than four lines of dialogue, and I promise that Hivju will steal every scene he’s in.

Runner-up: Olivier Richters for that Reacher season 3 fight scene alone.

Miles Surrey is a Brooklyn-based culture writer who covers television, film, and whatever your dad is interested in. His work can also be found at The Ringer, Vox, Vice, and The A.V. Club.
