New hypothesis explains why the right side of the human brain controls the left side of the body

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Automotive  |  Read Source →

The brain's crossed wiring runs through every vertebrate group, and two neuroscientists trace it to the eyes of the first fishes.

A stroke on the right side of the brain weakens the left arm and leg. The brain is cross-wired that way, and for more than a century scientists have assumed there was an evolutionary reason for it.

Two neuroscientists have traced the crossing through every major group of living vertebrates. They now propose that evolution may never have selected it for the jobs it does.

On their reading, the crossed wiring may simply be inherited from the way the first fishes’ eyes were wired to their brains, some 500 million years ago.

Martin Wessendorf, an associate professor emeritus of neuroscience at the University of Minnesota, and Daniel Wang, a researcher in the University’s Department of Neuroscience, wrote the review.

It is a hypothesis built from more than a century of published anatomy rather than a new experiment. However, the authors wrote that it may be testable in fossils.

In an interview with Earth.com, Wang said the question reaches beyond his own field.

“This’ll be relevant to anyone who wants to know more about why brain injury on one side of the head often leads to loss of function on the opposite side of the body,” he said.

The crossing begins at the eye. The lens flips the image, so whatever sits to your right lands on the left side of each retina. The nerve fibers carrying the right half of the view then sort themselves at a junction behind the eyes and run to the left side of the brain.

One place that information goes is a structure in the midbrain called the optic tectum. It holds a map of the opposite half of visual space, and it fires fast turning and escape movements.

Every vertebrate examined appears to have one, lampreys and hagfish included, along with the crossing junction behind the eyes.

Living jawless fish carry both, so the authors conclude that the last common ancestor of all vertebrates probably did too. That puts the first crossed wiring in the Cambrian period, among jawless fishes that lived roughly 538 to 486 million years ago.

Anatomists call crossed wiring contralateral organization, and its age is what struck Wang.

“What surprised me the most was likely just how old the contralateral organization of the optic tectum is for vertebrates – spanning even to the Cambrian,” he told Earth.com.

Body senses crossed next. Two pathways carry touch, pain, temperature and body position from the spinal cord up to the brain, and both cross the midline in amphibians, reptiles, birds and mammals.

The one for light touch and body position is missing in fishes, so the authors trace it to the last common ancestor of the four-limbed animals.

Movement pathways cross too. One runs from the optic tectum down to the spinal cord and turns the body toward whatever just appeared at the edge of vision.

Lampreys and bony fish appear to have no such pathway. Sharks have one that mostly stays on its own side, and rays and lungfish have a crossed one.

Reptiles appear to have lost that pathway. A second crossed pathway, from a cluster of midbrain cells called the red nucleus, is thought to help control limbs.

True mammals appeared about 165 million years ago. By then, those crossed senses and movement pathways had already existed for almost 200 million years.

The pathway that lets you move one finger at a time is the special case. It runs from the cerebral cortex, the six-layered outer sheet found only in mammals, down to the spinal cord, and it crosses in the lower brainstem.

In most mammals, it ends where touch signals enter the cord and adjusts those signals. Few mammals besides primates, raccoons and kinkajous have a version that reaches the nerve cells that drive muscles.

So the circuit that gives humans fine control of their hands appears to be a sensory circuit put to a new use. Wang said that was the finding that surprised his co-author most.

“This demonstrates that circuitry that’s involved in one function can, with just a change or two, become a function that’s entirely different.”

The crossing isn’t universal in this pathway. In goats and elephants, it runs down both sides of the spinal cord, and in hedgehogs and moles it mostly stays on its own side. Even in humans, an estimated 10 to 25 percent of its fibers don’t cross.

Wang pointed to the goats and elephants, “so there’s a decent amount of diversity there,” he said. The authors read that variety as a sign that natural selection wasn’t strongly favoring the crossing in this pathway.

Isaac Newton proposed in 1704 that the junction behind the eyes sorts nerve fibers by visual field.

Ramón y Cajal, who later won a Nobel Prize for his work on the structure of the nervous system, went further in an 1898 paper. Once the visual map crossed, he proposed, it would pay for the maps of hearing, touch and movement to match it.

In computer models published in 2014, an animal controlled by the opposite side of its brain was likelier to survive a blow to one side of the head or body.

At least two other research groups have blamed a twist in the body plan, dating to the split between vertebrates and their invertebrate ancestors. That argument goes back to an 1822 dissection of a crayfish.

The authors see a problem with the twist. The visual system was probably crossed in the first vertebrates, but the pathways for touch, limb control and finger control didn’t exist yet.

So the authors doubt that a single ancient twist can explain them.

The authors’ own answer starts with the optic tectum. Its map of the opposite half of visual space sits on top. Beneath it, in register, sit maps of touch and hearing from the same half, and at the bottom sit the cells that fire movements.

Wessendorf and Wang propose that this stack biased the sensory and motor pathways that evolved later toward crossing.

When the cortex evolved, most of the pathways carrying signals into it were already crossed. A cortex wired to its own side would have needed extra crossings to collect vision, touch and hearing from the far side, or a rewiring of every older circuit.

The authors argue that either change would have carried a “wiring cost,” leaving mammals that were less energy-efficient, less intelligent, or both.

Not everything crosses. Smell and taste mostly stay on the same side, and the authors note that neither sense maps space the way vision, hearing and touch do.

The cerebellum, which coordinates movement, works the same side of the body it sits on.

“We propose that contralateral organization didn’t necessarily evolve as an adaptation, but because evolution is an engineer that builds with the foundation and materials it has at hand,” Wang and Wessendorf wrote.

The authors list what would test the idea. Their first prediction is that the last common ancestor of vertebrates had both the crossing junction and an optic tectum.

Cambrian fossils of jawless fish exist, and a fossilized brain cast from one could show whether both were there.

Wang said the finding that would complicate the idea is an early fossil vertebrate whose optic tectum handled vision on the same side rather than the opposite one.

The authors also predict that crossed pathways from the forebrain to the spinal cord will prove widespread in birds. Unfortunately, relatively few bird species have been examined.

Crocodilians appear not to have been checked, and the authors predict they have one too.

Hearing is the one sense the authors treat with caution. It appears to have evolved partly on its own in different lines.

What remains unknown is whether any Cambrian fish skull preserves enough of its brain to show the crossing.

Until one does, the idea rests on living anatomy. The next checks are in the fossil record, and in the birds and crocodilians whose spinal pathways have yet to be mapped.

The full study was published in the journal Frontiers in Neuroanatomy.

Deutsche Bank under fire after internal documents made public

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

A cache of internal records outlines the bank's dealings with Jeffrey Epstein, Donald Trump and Russian oligarchs. The German lender has sought to deflect criticism by claiming its bad behavior is a thing of the past.

Deutsche Bank is in damage control mode again after an international consortium of more than 200 investigative journalists published internal documents from the global lender on Tuesday under the name "Inside Deutsche Bank."

The documents, published in Der Spiegel, Financial Times, Le Monde and The New York Times, among others, cover Deutsche Bank's business dealings with convicted sex offender Jeffrey Epstein, now US President Donald Trump, and a host of Russian oligarchs.

Gleaned from nearly 15 million documents leaked to a journalist from India and another from South Africa, the e-mails and meeting minutes trace the bank's aggressive pursuit of business deals in Russia, and with high-profile Russian oligarchs, right up until Moscow launched its full-scale invasion of Ukraine in 2022 — despite risk warnings from numerous managers.

Deutsche Bank CEO Christian Sewing was among the executives who took part in the bank's Russia strategy working group. The bank also still employs more than 160 people in Russia and generated 2025 pre-tax profits of €61 million ($67 million) there through a local subsidiary.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

In all, the documents cover the past 20 years of the German lender's business dealings and raise questions about the efficacy of internal controls by showing that Deutsche Bank had repeatedly put financial interests before international sanctions compliance and anti-money laundering rules.

Other red flags raised in Tuesday's reports suggest that Deutsche Bank sought to set up tax-optimization schemes for wealthy clients, that criminal networks may have been able to move illicit cash through the institution, that it helped facilitate arms deals, that it helped companies in environmentally damaging industries and that it held funds for the far-right populist Alternative for Germany (AfD) party.

Beyond more recent negative headlines involving alleged anti-money laundering and compliance failures, Deutsche Bank has been trying to restore its public image ever since its involvement in the US subprime mortgage rate scandal helped trigger the 2008 global financial crisis — a fiasco that ultimately cost the bank more than €20 billion in penalties and settlements.

When Sewing later took over in 2018, he promised to turn the institution around. Although he has made Deutsche Bank profitable once again, he did so at the cost of massive job cuts, and some compliance failings have continued.

Sewing to the German business daily Handelsblatt that some mistakes had been made under his leadership.

"When you look at the events today, you naturally ask yourself whether you could have done some things differently," he told the paper. "I am not without mistakes," he said, but added that the course he set for the bank "has always been right and remains so."

The bank's shares fell by up to 6% in Tuesday trading.

Deutsche Bank issued a statement claiming that the reports were old news and that the institution had long ago turned over a new leaf.

In an e-mailed statement to the press, Deutsche Bank Supervisory Board Chairman Alexander Wynaendts wrote, "During my tenure I haven't seen a single decision from Christian Sewing that I was not comfortable with."

A Tuesday statement from the bank described the revelations as "legacy" matters and claimed they would have no lasting negative financial impact on the institution.

Nevertheless, German Finance Minister and Vice Chancellor Lars Klingbeil on Tuesday called on Deutsche Bank to "provide full transparency regarding how it has addressed past failures and internal control deficiencies and how it will effectively prevent these in the future."

Further, Klingbeil said authorities must investigate and "take necessary action" where appropriate.

KERV.ai, Macmillan Publishers and Chomps are among this year’s Work/Life Awards finalists

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

This year’s Work/Life Awards finalists reflect a landscape where companies are rethinking workplace culture as a driver of business performance, employee engagement and innovation. Across industries, organizations are giving employees a more active role in shaping workplace policies and priorities, building recognition and connection into the everyday employee experience and extending their missions through community involvement and social impact.

KERV.ai earned a finalist spot in the Most Innovative Culture category for making employee input and cross-functional collaboration central to its workplace culture. Its quarterly HR listening tours, culture committee and real-time feedback programs gave employees opportunities to influence company policies and engagement initiatives. The emphasis on employee voice also extended to the business. Employees across product, engineering, client services and creative identified an opportunity to improve contextual targeting, leading to the development of Moment Match Engine, a video technology solution built through cross-departmental collaboration.

Macmillan Publishers is nominated in the Most Committed to Social Good category for connecting its workplace culture to a broader mission of expanding access to books, literacy and intellectual freedom. Through its MacImpact corporate social responsibility program, the publisher supports employee volunteering, charitable giving and community partnerships. In 2025, employees took part in 27 volunteer events and contributed 494 volunteer hours, a 60% increase from the previous year. Its employee-led Yours to Read Committee also advocates for access to diverse literature and the freedom to read.

Chomps, a finalist for Most Committed to Employee Appreciation, focused on maintaining employee connection as its remote-first workforce grew to more than 200 people across 30-plus states. The company brings employees together through annual retreats, departmental offsites and company-wide gatherings, while recognition programs such as Chompian of the Quarter and Chompian of the Year celebrate employee contributions. Investments in wellness, mental health days and professional development further extend appreciation beyond formal recognition.

Together, this year’s nominees represent a workplace landscape in which culture is shaped by employee participation, meaningful recognition and a commitment to a broader purpose beyond business goals. As expectations of employers continue to evolve, their approaches offer examples of how organizations can build more connected, engaged and purpose-driven workplaces.

Tulum Is Losing Airlines Fast: Why The Hyped New Airport Is Failing

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: World  |  Read Source →

In late 2023, we saw the opening of the Felipe Carrillo Puerto International Airport (TQO), in Tulum, Mexico. For those not familiar, Tulum is located on the eastern coast of the Yucatan Peninsula, and for years, it was a growing tourist destination.

While it could previously be accessed via Cancun International Airport (CUN), the hope was that by opening a new international airport, visitor numbers would expand massively. Unfortunately what has actually happened is exactly the opposite, as airline after airline has pulled out. What exactly is going on here? I think it makes for an interesting story, and thanks to TravelinWilly for flagging this.

In 2023, we saw airline after airline announce flights to Tulum. Among US carriers, we saw American, Delta, JetBlue, and United, all launch flights to Tulum, while Spirit announced flights, but never launched them.

That was only a small part of the overall international service to the airport, as we also saw flights to Tulum on Air Canada, Air Transat, Avianca, Copa, Discover, and WestJet, along with flights on domestic carriers like Aeromexico, Mexicana de Aviacion, Viva, and Volaris.

Less than three years after the airport opened, where do things stand? When it comes to international year-round service, we’ve now gone from service to 12 airports, to service to four airports, which is an absolutely massive reduction:

Just to put this into numbers, in the first half of 2026, the airport saw a 33.2% reduction in passengers. June 2026 was the worst month to date, with a 44.8% drop, as there were just 47,600 passengers, compared to 86,200 passengers the year prior.

It goes without saying that for a newly built airport, this is rather catastrophic, and not what you hope to see.

In some ways, it feels like the whole concept behind Tulum Airport was sort of unnecessary. That’s also easily explained by the fact that the airport was well over a decade in the making, so while I can see how it might’ve seemed like a good idea at some point, tourism trends do evolve over time.

The reality is that there was a point where Tulum was cool, edgy, and a bit off the beaten path, compared to… whatever it is now. But when it comes to the struggles that airlines have had with flying to Tulum, it seems like the problems have been endless:

It’ll be interesting to see if Tulum can reverse any of these trends, and regain visitors…

A few years ago Tulum Airport opened to some fanfare, as a good number of airlines rushed to add service there. Unfortunately the excitement didn’t last long, as the airport has gone from a dozen year-round international routes, to just four.

This reduction in demand particularly evident from the United States, as soon there will just be nonstop service on American and Delta, and even that will be with reduced frequencies and routes.

Tulum Airport is facing endless challenges, not the least of which is a decline in visitors to Mexico from the United States, combined with increasing competition for those fewer visitors. Mexico just isn’t a value destination in the way that it used to be in places like Cancun, Tulum, Los Cabos, etc., and that’s understandably impacting demand.

I’m curious if Tulum can find a way to turn things around, but it’s not a good situation.

What do you make of the Tulum Airport demand problem?

Stronger Together: HomeServices of America Celebrates Top Agents, Fuels Future Growth

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Above: HomeServices of America President and CEO Chris Kelly addresses the crowd at the company’s “Stronger Together” Conference

While real estate success is often measured individually—with an emphasis on closed transactions, milestones achieved and accolades earned—some of the greatest opportunities for growth present themselves when real estate professionals come together.

That philosophy was at the heart of HomeServices of America’s 4th Annual “Stronger Together” Conference, held Oct. 26 – 29 at the Fontainebleau Miami Beach. The oceanside event brought together more than 400 agents from the HomeServices’ owned brokerage companies to celebrate the professionals whose achievements earned them a place among the company’s best—each invited on the strength of their success.

Collectively, these agents rank in the top 1.5% of approximately 1.4 million Realtors® nationwide—averaging seven times the sales volume of the national average and three times more transactions. Generating $10.2 billion in annual sales volume, Stronger Together agent attendees are responsible for helping 16,483 families move each year.

“Our agents earned their place at Stronger Together, and we wanted every moment to reflect how much we value them,” HomeServices of America President and CEO Chris Kelly said in a release.

The mastermind behind the reinvigoration of the organization, Kelly has led HomeServices of America’s transformation from a quiet holding company into a bold parent company focused on providing strategic direction, cohesion and resources across its family of brokerage, mortgage, insurance and title services.

Central to that vision is a commitment to investing in people, performance and long-term growth that extends throughout the organization—a commitment reflected in reflections and anecdotes shared with RISMedia after the event from both HomeServices of America executives and attendees alike.

Playing a key role in educating and elevating the performance of brokerage owners, operators, team leaders, office managers and the sales associates in the field each and every day, HomeServices of America Chief Coaching Officer Jimmy Burgess takes pride in seeing that work come together.

“I’ve been part of this event for a long time, and what still gets me is the room. Every person in it earned their seat, and they’re all generous with what they know,” says Burgess, who spent the week watching agents trade ideas between sessions as much as in them.

“It’s a privilege to say thank you to people who work this hard, and to send them home with a few new ideas and a few new friends,” he adds.

Reflecting the company’s commitment to delivering the complete real estate experience—a theme central to HomeServices of America’s strategy—this year’s event mirrored that philosophy by offering agents inspiration, practical insight and community in one setting. Newly introduced Super Sessions, which go beyond a traditional breakout or panel, and inspiring voices provided top-performing agents everything they need to keep winning in today’s market.

For clients, that complete experience is delivered through OnePoint, the integrated platform HomeServices of America launched earlier this year to provide a more streamlined, end-to-end experience to buyers and sellers alike.

“We changed a few things this year, including the Super Sessions and a main stage with three voices instead of one, because folks at this level already know their craft. What they want is room to keep growing,” explains Kelly.

“It’s the same thinking behind OnePoint: Give people what they need in one place, so they aren’t piecing it together alone,” he adds. “When our agents grow, their clients feel it.”

Providing agents the tools and motivation to keep succeeding, entrepreneur, investor and bestselling author Jesse Itzler kicked off the conference with a keynote presentation challenging attendees to redefine wealth through experiences, relationships, mindset, health and purpose.

In addition to Super Sessions designed to give agents the time and depth to explore a multitude of topics impacting the industry, attendees also heard shared perspectives on kindness, connection and determination. Another Stronger Together highlight was a live recording of the “Stay Paid” podcast with Kelly, Burgess and Prosperity Home Mortgage President and CEO Justin Messer.

“A gathering like this sets a standard for the industry,” says HomeServices of America Senior Vice President Lacey Conway. “With more than 400 agents in the top 1.5% nationally, the program had to match the people in the room, and it did.

“From the keynote to the ‘Super Sessions’ to the speakers on (the topic of) belonging, you could see the same things we see in these agents: Curiosity, discipline and a willingness to keep learning when they’re already at the top,” she says.

HomeServices of America is putting that same forward-thinking mindset into action across the board.

“What stands out about HomeServices is that it keeps building while it’s winning, and OnePoint is a good example. Bringing brokerage, mortgage, title and insurance together as one coordinated team is a real answer to what clients want,” says HomeServices of America Chief Marketing and Strategy Officer Brian Boero, who led one of the conference’s Super Sessions.

“The energy in the room at Stronger Together had that same appetite for what’s next…and that’s a sign of a company that plans to keep leading,” he adds.

The value of coming together: Ideas, connections and new perspectives

Attending HomeServices of America’s Stronger Together event along with 15 top-producing agents, Long Companies CEO Kevin Kaplan says that being able to bring together hundreds of the most successful real estate professionals from across the HomeServices network to learn, share ideas and build relationships is what ultimately makes the conference so valuable.

“What sets Stronger Together apart is that the focus goes beyond traditional sales skills and tactics. It’s about mindset, leadership, motivation and challenging yourself to think differently about your business and what’s possible,” says Kaplan, who believes that surrounding yourself with successful people who challenge and inspire you is one of the greatest opportunities for growth.

“Our agents came away energized, with new ideas, valuable connections and fresh perspectives on how to take their businesses to the next level,” he adds. “In my view, this was the best ‘Stronger Together’ event yet, and we’re already looking forward to 2027.”

For Elizabeth Nunan, president and CEO of Houlihan Lawrence, the conference was equally as powerful.

“Stronger Together was an outstanding conference from start to finish. The speakers were exceptional, the content was timely and relevant, and the opportunities to connect, network and share ideas made it even more valuable,” she says.

“Our agents left feeling inspired, energized and excited about what’s ahead. In fact, they’re already making plans for next year in Nashville. That’s always a pretty good indication of a great event,” adds Nunan.

For RISMedia Founder and CEO John Featherston, what stood out most was the energy and the positive, can-do attitude throughout the event.

“I had the privilege of attending the 4th Annual Stronger Together conference, and watching the attendees connect with so many other passionate real estate professionals reminded me just how valuable it is to meet face-to-face and collaborate with industry leaders to share knowledge and techniques—and why it’s so important to success in our industry,” says Featherston, who applauded Kelly and his team for delivering an informative, meaningful and well-received experience for attendees.

“When you couple the industry talents educating attendees—from Jimmy Burgess’ coaching insights and Brian Boero’s informative, practical information—with incredible guests such as serial entrepreneur and best-selling author Jesse Itzler and award-winning singer, songwriter and author Nicolle Galyon along with several other incredible speakers—Stronger Together was a smashing success,” he adds.

“Congratulations to Chris and his team for a job well done!”

The Mighty Nein Season 2 Officially Has Fans Worried About One Major Plot Change

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Prime Video teases a pirate-soaked second season, but will some major developments be left behind?

The Mighty Nein season 2 is officially set to return to our TV screens in March 2027. And while Critical Role fans may be a little put out by Amazon's new episode schedule, the season’s content has Critters excited for what’s to come from our band of lovable misfits.

However, there does seem to be a significant catch: Season 2 may be widening the gap between the show and the original Dungeons & Dragons campaign even further than season 1 did. While the first season already moved away from the source campaign in several ways, the second season could be taking those departures even further.

[Ed. note: This article contains spoilers for the tabletop campaign of The Mighty Nein.]

Prime Video’s announcement for season 2 of The Mighty Nein gave Critters a glimpse of what to expect. You can read the summary below.

“In Season Two, The Mighty Nein set off on a pirate adventure across the high seas as they face their complicated pasts and hurtle toward a treacherous future. While Fjord’s burgeoning powers tempt him down a dark path, the Beacon reveals its own enticing possibilities, and the group must choose between newfound magic and their friendship.”

On the surface, it looks like business as usual for the Mighty Nein (there are only seven of them, but we digress). However, some eagle-eyed Critters who have watched the original 141 episodes of the tabletop campaign have started to speculate what this new plot summary could mean for The Mighty Nein season 2’s story.

Last time, Critters left off with the Mighty Nein encountering the aasimar barbarian Yasha in the Zadash sewers after stealing the Luxon Beacon. Briefly free from the mind-control of Obann, a cambion, Yasha pleaded with the group to help her.

Though season 1 has already introduced several differences — for example, Yasha wasn’t introduced alongside Mollymauk as part of the Fletching and Moondrop Traveling Carnival of Curiosities troupe — the new season’s summary, combined with season 1’s ending, has fans speculating that the Iron Shepherds arc may be skipped entirely in favor of jumping straight to the Mighty Nein’s pirate arc.

The Iron Shepherds arc is a formative experience for the group, as it comes shortly after the Mighty Nein are given a task by the Gentleman (a crime boss in the city of Zadash) to head to Shadycreek Run and retrieve an ally of his. However, on the way there, the group is ambushed in the middle of the night, resulting in the capture of Fjord, Jester, and Yasha by a group of slavers known as The Iron Shepherds. When the rest of the group tries to deal with the slavers, they find themselves overwhelmed, with Mollymauk dying in the process.

The plot summary of Season 2, however, suggests nothing of the sort; its main focus is the pirate arc that comes afterward. Now, this could be Critical Role and Prime Video choosing to obscure the Iron Shepherds arc entirely to surprise new fans with what’s to come. That said, given how many changes Critical Role has already made to the campaign — and for what it’s worth, we think they are for the better — there’s also a chance the Iron Shepherds' encounter will be woven into the pirate arc. After all, the Mighty Nein make plenty of enemies during that arc, too, including Captain Avantika, who plays a major role in Fjord’s storyline. Could she be the one who ends up slaughtering Mollymauk?

It’s hard to predict what changes are coming to The Mighty Nein season 2, but given how much the first season changed from the original campaign, Critters should brace for even more in March 2027.

The Mighty Nein airs exclusively on Prime Video. Season 1 is available to watch now, with season 2 set to debut on March 24, 2027.

Huddersfield Town told to make January transfer move for ‘aggressive’ Barnsley star

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Optimism is beginning to build that Huddersfield Town can gain promotion and finally make their return to the Championship.

The Terriers currently sit at the summit of the third tier standings after taking 29 points from their opening 14 league games, while having a game in hand on some of their promotion rivals after not playing last weekend.

It had been a disappointing last couple of weeks in Yorkshire after Huddersfield were held at home in the league by Luton Town to end a run of four successive victories, while they also suffered defeat in the FA Cup at the hands of Exeter City, going down 2-1.

Martin Drury’s men were able to respond in their final group game of the EFL Trophy last week however, putting Liverpool's U21’s to the sword in a 4-0 victory that helped them top Northern Group H.

League action finally returns for Huddersfield this coming weekend, with the Terriers hosting Leicester City in a massive game for both teams towards the top end of the table.

Meanwhile, to solidify their place in the promotion picture after a hectic run of winter fixtures, the January transfer window could be the perfect time to make a statement signing and highlight their intent of returning to the second tier this season.

With that in mind, FLW’s Huddersfield Town fan pundit has backed the Terriers to complete a raid of a fellow Yorkshire club for an ‘aggressive’ star.

Much of Huddersfield Town’s excellent form leading into the festive period has been down to their recruitment in the forward areas, with the Terriers looking so much more potent compared to their previous couple of seasons in League One.

The addition of Derensilli Sanches Fernandes looks inspired at the moment, with the creative Cape Verde midfielder contributing seven goals and six assists from 14 matches, while the signing of frontman Ashley Fletcher has added much-needed experience and allowed Martin Drury to chop and change between him or Joe Taylor leading the line, who himself has 10 goals in 13 league games.

Martin Drury wasn't on the touchline for Huddersfield's recent EFL Trophy win, and the reasoning has sparked a reaction amongst the fanbase

Their quality moving forward and doggedness in their defensive duties has seen them lose just once all season so far in the league, with eight wins and five draws collected from their other 13 outings.

There’s such a feel-good factor at the Accu Stadium at the moment, with Huddersfield fans in awe of the job Drury is doing that could finally see them be back in the Championship setup next season.

Speaking to Football League World, Huddersfield Town fan pundit Graeme Rayner has urged the Terriers to raid fellow Yorkshire outfit Barnsley for winger Reyes Cleary in January, believing the addition of the 22-year-old can help the team be even more dangerous in attack, and that the player has ‘all the attributes’ to help the club achieve a top two spot come the end of the campaign.

When asked who Huddersfield should try and sign in January, Graeme told FLW: “I think it’s a player I’ve mentioned before in the summer, I would love us to take a really good hard look at Reyes Cleary, who plays for Barnsley.

“He’s a left-winger, 22-years-old, was the top assister in League One last year. He was playing in a pretty poor Barnsley side and still managed 15 assists and seven goals in 50 appearances.

“This season, he’s playing in a Barnsley side that are right at the foot of the table, they’re joint-bottom of the league, and he’s scored a couple and assisted one in a team that’s only scored 12 goals, he’s contributed in a way to a quarter of those goals.

“He’s quick, he’s capable, he’s clearly got a good finish on him, he’s aggressive, I think he’s six-foot, he’s a big unit, and got every kind of attribute that we’d want from someone, and what we lack at the moment is a really good option on the left that would give us what Derensili Sanches Fernandes gives us on the right.

Huddersfield Town fans, would you welcome a move for Reyes Cleary in January? Could he be the signing that helps get you over the line for promotion? Drop your thoughts below!

“In our front three we’re quite often playing Fernandes and then a big man, little man combo with an Ashley Fletcher, or Bojan Radulovic, alongside (Joe) Taylor. What this would give us is the option to play Fernandes and someone like Reyes, with Taylor through the middle, and I think we’d be even more dangerous, and he (Cleary) would be the exciting player that could help us get over the line for automatic promotion.

“I think he’s got capabilities and potential to play at a higher level. If he’s doing that well in a pretty abject, woeful side, then he could do really well in a team that are playing on the front foot as much as we are these days.

“He goes hunting for the ball, he closes down defenders, he wins the ball, he’s a good finisher, he’s got all the attributes that we need, and he wouldn’t be hugely expensive, I wouldn’t have thought.

“He’s been at Barnsley for a season and a half, his history is not many games at West Brom where he came through the youth setup I believe, half a season with Hartlepool and then onto Barnsley, so he’s not going to be a massively expensive signing, maybe a million quid would do it? I don’t know.

“So, I say let’s have a look at him. He could be really exciting.”

As another transfer window rolls around, the future of Reyes Cleary hangs in the balance, and Huddersfield Town might be the club to finally take advantage.

Cleary had been tipped to depart Oakwell in the summer, but elected to remain with the Reds, but after a diabolical start to the season that leaves them joint-bottom of the division, Cleary may well want to move on and link up with a promotion-contending outfit to then have a route into playing Championship football next season.

And Huddersfield would seem the perfect place, with the Terriers currently so dangerous in the forward areas and having a line that rips apart opposition defences.

Not to mention that a left-winger is the one area that Huddersfield are missing at the moment despite having such an impressive attack, and you feel if that is addressed, then there is no way of stopping them blowing away most of their opponents and dominating the division for the rest of the campaign.

While Cleary’s numbers have been nowhere near the levels he managed to notch last season with Barnsley, you have to feel that is because he is playing in such a struggling side, and those returns would surely get back to their best when playing in a forward line that is so front-footed and dominant.

Huddersfield’s current setup would be a perfect one for Cleary to come into, with the 22-year-old able to use his pace, power, directness and clinical eye for goal to great effect, and it could be the missing piece that makes Huddersfield a shoo-in for promotion back to the Championship.

If the Terriers were able to pull this deal off in January, then their chances of automatic promotion are bound to considerably increase, and it’s a piece of transfer business that the club must get over the line if they are serious about achieving promotion.

We want to hear from you. Share your perspective in the comments below, and please keep the conversation respectful.

Amflow’s new urban e-bike series is deliciously overpowered for flat city streets

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

After shaking up the mountain bike world, DJI spinoff Amflow is hitting city streets with the UE Carbon and UE Carbon Pro — bringing 125Nm of torque, 82 miles of range, and Find My tracking — starting at $3,500.

After shaking up the mountain bike world, DJI spinoff Amflow is hitting city streets with the UE Carbon and UE Carbon Pro — bringing 125Nm of torque, 82 miles of range, and Find My tracking — starting at $3,500.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

DJI spinoff Amflow has spent the last couple of years shaking up the electric mountain bike (eMTB) market with ridiculously high-torque, lightweight models. Today, it’s bringing that same overengineered energy to pavement with the UE Series, its first urban commuter line.

The series consists of two models: the UE Carbon and UE Carbon Pro. Both pack 125Nm of torque, 1,100W of peak power, and an 8x max assist ratio capable of climbing 30 percent grades — all while operating quietly under 45dBA (quieter than a household refrigerator). A swappable 600Wh battery provides up to 132km (about 82 miles) of range and fast-charges from zero to 100 percent in 2 hours and 23 minutes.

While most city e-bikes deliver their power modestly, Amflow is strapping a mountain-bike-grade motor onto a 20.9 kg (46 pounds) upright step-through frame — and pricing it slightly below its flagship trail bikes. The UE Carbon starts at €3,099 (about $3,500 USD), while the Pro will sell for €3,499 ($3,900 USD).

But this isn’t just another commuter bike. With its proprietary Avinox drivetrain, DJI has been ahead of the curve in blending consumer gadget UX — features like wireless phone charging, Apple Watch proximity unlock, Find My integration, and camera controls — with serious hardware specs. That’s one of the reasons why The Verge’s own Thomas Ricker has been so bullish on the brand for the past few years.

With 125Nm of max torque and 1,100W of peak power, the Avinox M2 in the UE Series is slightly more dialed back from Amflow’s 150Nm flagship eMTBs. That said, 125Nm is still massive overkill for flat city streets — but it won’t hurt to have it when encountering any grades over 30 percent. A new Auto Boost mode automatically dumps extra power when starting at a traffic light or hitting a steep hill. And traction control will help prevent any spin-outs on wet asphalt.

The 600Wh removable battery is good for up 82 miles in Eco mode — and likely less than that while relying on higher levels of assist. It’s worth noting that the 1,100W peak power and 8x assist ratios will likely be software-capped depending on where you live and what regulations allow.

As a brand within the larger DJI ecosystem, Amflow loves to integrate a lot of tech into its bikes. A 1.4 inch touchscreen is integrated into the handlebars alongside a 15W wireless phone charger — plus a 20W USB-C charger, if you prefer. The cockpit also features plenty of Apple tie-ins, like the aforementioned Find My tracker and Apple Watch proximity unlocking. The UE can also pair with a heart rate monitor, allowing riders to set a target heart rate zone that will dictate how much pedal assist the bike provides. And, of course, there’s direct recording controls for DJI Osmo cameras, in case you like to film your rides.

Additional security is provided by a built-in 10mm hardened steel wheel lock keyed to match the removable battery. And if the bike is paired with “Lost Mode” in the Amflow app, the user can remotely brick the drive system if the bike ever gets stolen.

There’s plenty of commuter-friendly touches, like a 65mm suspension fork, suspension dropper seatpost, and heavy-duty racks rated for 20kg (44 pounds) on the front and 27kg (60 pounds) on the rear.

The bikes are on sale globally starting today via Amflow’s website and retail partners. The company says a smaller XS frame size is coming in 2027 for shorter riders.

UK's Health Service Issues Warning Over GTA 6, Urges Players to Take Care of Their Hands

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

GTA 6 is so massive that even the UK's National Health Service — commonly known as the NHS — has gotten in on the act.

To be fair, the NHS raises a good point with regards to the open world game's imminent release. On Instagram, it's urging players to take regular breaks in order to avoid issues like repetitive strain injury — or RSI for short.

In a cruel twist, the official account's admin has actually had to undergo surgery to fix their problem, meaning that playing GTA 6 at launch is going to be difficult.

"Thoughts and prayers for your admin. Bit of surgery to remove a lump. Great NHS service, bad timing," the post reads.

"Planning on dedicating time to gaming later this week? Try and take regular breaks, obviously. And be mindful of RSI," it continues.

In this case, RSI is caused by overuse of certain muscles in the hands and fingers, brought about by repeated actions. It's a pretty common injury amongst gamers, and without wanting to scare you off completely, it can lead to lasting damage if you refuse to properly rest the impacted area.

The NHS has got a whole page on RSI if you want more information.

Have you ever had an RSI? Remember to pause your game in the comments section below.

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

I know it, you know it, hell, I'm sure developer Archetype Entertainment know it: Exodus is kind of just Mass Effect again. That's in part because the DNA of the game has been built by series vets like Drew Karpyshyn, Chad Robertson and James Ohlen. But it's also because, well, they're putting FemShep in the game. Yes, I'm being a tad facetious here, but when the latest trailer for your game is literally titled "Female Jun Reveal," well, comparisons will be made!

To top it all off, a blog post from the devs literally refers to the alternate gender for the protag as FemJun. They know what they're doing. In any case, first up, her voice actor! She's being played by Carina Conti, an up and comer last digitally heard in Mafia: The Old Country as Isabella Torrisi. Male Jun (who hasn't been given a nickname, which couldn't possibly serve to other female Jun (and MascJun is right there!)) has also had his voice actor confirmed as Stephen Oyoung, who you'll have heard as Mister Negative in Insomniac's Spider-Man games and Red Hood in Gotham Knights.

It's been previously confirmed that no matter which gender you pick for Jun, you'll be able to romance whoever you like, so, happy smoochin'! The story will stay the same too, as will dialogue options, though I'm imagine some pronoun switcheroos will take place somewhere along the line.

I'm mostly just left feeling that this doesn't really seem like it'll fix the game's boring hero problem. Edwin made note of Jun's quite lacklustre vibe earlier this year, aptly tearing down MascJun's Instagram visage, though this was prior to the face model and voice actor change the character has undergone. Personally, I'm not sure that'll be enough, and FemJun kind of matches that Instagram look; the perfect bun and bangs that pop out when she takes her helmet off is quite silly too. Ah well! A game isn't done until it's done (which, in theory, will be April 7th next year).

Report: Six clubs interested in Milan midfielder Jashari including four domestic rivals

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Other  |  Read Source →

A number of clubs are interested in signing Ardon Jashari from AC Milan, according to a report from his native Switzerland.

Jashari has once again found himself playing largely as a back-up under Ruben Amorim this season, after performing the role of being Luka Modric’s deputy during his debut campaign with the Rossoneri under Massimiliano Allegri.

Read SempreMilan ad-free and get access to exclusive news. Click here for a free trial!

Given that over €30m was invested to sign him from Club Brugge, a bigger role and impact was perhaps foreseen by all parties. Thus, Jashari’s situation is one to monitor, and there is certainly no shortage of interested parties.

According to information from 4-4-2.ch, clubs in both Serie A and the Premier League are seriously considering a move for the 24-year-old midfielder, who started last night in Switzerland’s 4-0 win over Scotland and impressed.

Roma, Juventus, Atalanta and Como are the four Italian clubs that are looking at the Swiss international. From England, it is Aston Villa and Nottingham Forest that are monitoring his situation closely.

Jashari is under contract until 2030, so Milan have leverage, and they have not yet received a concrete offer. However, this could change as January gets closer. The Rossoneri still highly value Jashari, even though he hasn’t yet established himself as a regular starter.

A permanent sale would therefore require a fee in the range of €35-40m. Milan paid Club Brugge a similar sum for him a year ago and they have no intention of making a loss, given his age and potential.

Bayern Munich are reportedly stepping up their efforts to sign Liverpool youngster Rio Ngumoha, but the Premier League giants remain determined to keep hold of the highly-rated winger despite growing interest from Germany.

The 18-year-old has emerged as one of the most exciting young prospects in English football, attracting attention from several European heavyweights following his impressive development at Anfield.

According to a TEAMtalk report, Bayern have recently contacted Ngumoha’s representatives to explore bringing him to the Allianz Arena.

The Bundesliga champions are keen to strengthen their attacking options and have identified the Liverpool youngster as a potential addition. However, convincing the Merseyside club to sanction his departure will be extremely difficult.

Liverpool have no intention of letting the teenager leave during the January transfer window, viewing him as an important part of their long-term project.

Ngumoha has already made 44 appearances for the senior side, contributing two goals and three assists. His pace, dribbling ability and confidence in one-on-one situations have made him an exciting prospect for the future.

The Reds are particularly encouraged by his development and believe he has the potential to become one of Europe’s leading attacking talents.

Furthermore, Liverpool are working towards securing his long-term future with an improved contract. Although an agreement has not yet been reached, discussions are progressing.

Bayern are not the only European giants monitoring his situation, with Real Madrid and Paris Saint-Germain also previously linked with the youngster.

However, Liverpool’s position remains firm, and it would take an extraordinary change in circumstances for them to consider a departure. The Anfield hierarchy would much rather build around Ngumoha than allow another European powerhouse to benefit from his development.

Amazon CEO Andy Jassy feared being ‘a lousy dad’—so he did this

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Other  |  Read Source →

When Amazon CEO Andy Jassy became a parent for the first time 26 years ago, he didn’t want to choose between being a good worker and being a good father.

That left him with only one option.

“I realized that I had to change the way I worked,” Jassy recently said during an appearance at Amazon’s Bring Your Parents to Work Day. “[Otherwise,] I was either going to have to leave Amazon, or I was going to be a lousy dad.”

Jassy was used to working grueling hours—often staying at the office late into the night—and he wasn’t sure if he could do his job effectively on a tighter schedule.

“I didn’t know how I would do my job without [working all the time],” he said. “But like everything else, if you think about it and you rework it and you find ways to be more efficient and you make trade-offs, you can do it.”

One step was to ensure that, whenever he was at the office, he was using his time efficiently.

“I did spend a lot of time in the hallway talking to people, and I wasn’t always prioritizing the things that mattered most,” Jassy said. “It forced me to get more efficient in what I did.”

He also established rules to help him prioritize family: being home for dinner every day, having weekly breakfasts with each of his children, and maintaining a standing Tuesday date night with his wife.

The challenge Jassy faced will likely be familiar to any parent struggling to balance career ambitions with responsibilities at home. And it is increasingly common among men, a growing number of whom say they are interested in being parents or caregivers. 

In one recent survey, over two-thirds of men said they consider caregiving to be as important as paid work and that both parents should contribute equally—yet only 40% of men who are caregivers said they shared the responsibility equally with their partners.

In the same survey, nearly two-thirds of male caregivers said work obligations interfered with their family time. A third reported having “no control” over their schedules, and only 40% said they had a hybrid or remote work arrangement. (Amazon issued a five-day RTO policy in 2024.)

Meanwhile, the study found that male caregivers are increasingly missing work or cutting back on their work hours. The percentage of men who care for adults or children with extra needs who reported missing work rose from 64% in 2019 to 76% in 2026.  

And, as Fast Company reported recently, men in some states are filing at least as many claims for parental leave as mothers.

For fathers who want to keep their career ambitions and still be equal parents, Jassy said there may be no one-size-fits-all solution.

“There are times you have to be flexible with what’s happening in your life and at work,” he said. “But it’s doable if you want to do it.”

Ex-goalkeeper names England's 'most suited' number one out of Jordan Pickford and James Trafford

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Leeds United number one James Trafford should be Thomas Tuchel's first choice in goal for England ahead of Everton star Jordan Pickford.

That is according to former senior Everton scout and ex-Millwall and Coventry City goalkeeper, Bryan King, speaking exclusively to Football Insider.

The Leeds goalkeeper started on Sunday as England beat Spain 3-2 in Madrid, making several key saves in the second half as the 10-man visitors held on.

Trafford left Manchester City for the Whites in the summer transfer window, joining in a deal worth up to £45million after he had made just 17 appearances under Pep Guardiola.

Now the undisputed number one at Elland Road, the 24-year-old looks to be putting long-term England goalkeeper Pickford under serious pressure.

The 32-year-old has 94 England caps and has never let his country down, but may now face a battle for his position.

England’s number one? 🤔A look back at some of James Trafford’s saves from last night 🔥 pic.twitter.com/BLEMO1YAsD

While it is debatable if Trafford is a better shot stopper than Pickford, there is no arguing about the difference in quality in build-up.

The 24-year-old is a natural with the ball at his feet - a quality that Tuchel may find hard to resist.

"I look at how many caps Pickford has won and I just shake my head. He's got 94 caps and that's basically because there's been no real opposition for him," King told Football Insider.

"[Dean] Henderson looked as though he could provide that opposition, but he's playing in a side where the team makes mistakes. That leads to poor goals going in.

"[Nick] Pope just disappeared off the face of the planet, you know, number three at Newcastle. [Aaron] Ramsdale looked as though he was going to be the business at Arsenal.

"Arsenal were too big for him. Emirates Stadium was too big for him. Went to Southampton, did okay, still there. He'll never been an England goalkeeper again.

"But then you start to think, who is there? You can't name today five top English goalkeepers. At the moment, Trafford is the most suited man for the job.

"He's a very down-to-earth boy, isn't he? I like him. I like his attitude. He's quiet. He gets on with his job. He's a Gordon Banks."

England had been cruising against the world champions until Elliot Anderson's red card in the 63rd minute.

Spain converted the penalty and scored 10 minutes later through Nico Williams, setting up a grandstand finish.

Trafford made six saves and prevented 2.57 goals in the closing stages, including a spectacular double save to deny Fermin Lopez and Aymeric Laporte.

Even if the Leeds man has not earned the shirt yet, he has almost certainly opened the conversation.

Ayumu Imazu Talks ‘musubi’ & The Belief that Music ‘Brings People Together’: Billboard Japan Monthly Interview

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: World  |  Read Source →

He chatted with Billboard Japan about the making of the song & what he envisions for the Budokan and beyond.

Singer/songwriter Ayumu Imazu spoke to Billboard Japan for its Monthly Feature interview series spotlighting artists and releases on its radar.

Imazu’s latest single “musubi,” released digitally on Oct. 2, has become the biggest hit of his career. “musubi” is a love song portraying the bonds between people through the motif of the “red string of fate.” A demo posted to TikTok in late August, when only a single verse and chorus existed, spread widely and led to an “emergency release.” The track swept the charts across streaming services immediately after it dropped and earned the ​26-year-old artist his first-ever No. 1 on Billboard Japan’s Streaming Songs chart (dated Oct. 14, 2026), marking the biggest breakthrough of his career to date.

2026 has also been a milestone year for him — it marks the fifth anniversary of his major-label debut, he announced a partnership with SKY-HI-led BMSG in February, and in May he released CLASSIC, his first album in four years. Next up, on Jan. 31, 2027, he’ll play his first-ever headlining show at Nippon Budokan, a venue he has long named as a goal.  Related Wale on Triumphant Pair of 2027 Grammy Nominations for 'Everything Is a Lot': 'I Hope It Teaches a Lot of People About Resilience' Miley Cyrus Reacts to Shared Grammy Nod With Dolly Parton: 'A Beautiful Way to Honor Her Enormous Heart' Rosalía Reacts to Her 9 Grammy Nominations: See How She Celebrated Explore Billboard See latest videos, charts and news

After pouring all of himself into CLASSIC, “musubi” emerged as Imazu circled back to the foundation of his music, which is about “bringing people together.” He chatted with Billboard Japan about the making of the song, the inspiration he’s drawn from his new environment at BMSG, and what he envisions for the Budokan and beyond.

“musubi” has been generating a huge response. When did you start working on it?

Around two months ago, in mid-August. I finished the first verse and chorus at the end of August and posted it on TikTok maybe two days after that.

The title is symbolic. “musubi” is a love song about the bonds that tie people together. Where did the idea come from?

In May​,​ I released CLASSIC, my first album in four years, and most of it was new songs I co-wrote with overseas producers. I put so much into making it that I felt pretty empty in a way afterward. I was gradually taking things in and building my energy back up while touring, and as I considered what to make next, I vaguely thought it’d be nice to do something about bringing people together, because I feel that’s the biggest purpose of making music. I started making it with that as the big goal.

Bringing people together through music — is that one of the motivations you’ve always held onto?

Maybe not always, but with the way the times are going… right now I feel like everyone’s own worlds of the things they like are scattered all over the place. It’s becoming harder for everyone to get excited about a single piece of music or to share the same feelings. That’s why I wanted to make a song like “musubi.”

You announced your partnership with BMSG this February, and in April you also shared that you’d gotten married. Do you feel those changes in your working environment and life stage have influenced you?

I wasn’t thinking about those things in particular. More than anything, I really wanted to make something that carried on from the album. I put out CLASSIC after forming the partnership with BMSG, wanting to show the fullest extent of myself all over again, like, “This is Ayumu Imazu.” So doing the same thing didn’t feel right. My next focus naturally turned to what’s at the core of my music, the theme of connecting people.

CLASSIC was an album built around powerful, polished dance numbers that were quintessential Ayumu Imazu. At the same time, vocal-driven songs that showcase the beauty of the Japanese language, like “Anata To Itai” and “musubi,” are essential to any discussion of your discography. How do you think about balancing or blending the two?

When I was first starting out and trying to show people who Ayumu Imazu is as an artist, I was really conflicted between going with dance tracks and the fact that I also love ballads. But my strongest feeling was that I didn’t want to choose (just one), so after putting out all kinds of songs, I now feel the dance tracks are very Ayumu Imazu, and I also really feel that slow-tempo songs like “musubi” are me, too. The range of musical styles is broad, but I don’t really feel like I’m moving away from the foundation of my own feelings and personality.

Does your actual production process change depending on the style of the song?

It does. With dance tracks​,​ I usually start with the beat, and with ballad-type songs like this one, I usually work out the chords on acoustic guitar or piano, write the melody to a certain point, and then program it.

What did you focus on most in terms of your vocals?

The demo was already out there and everyone had gotten used to hearing it, so when I recorded the final take, I had to either make it sound similar or surpass it. I’d gotten used to hearing it myself, especially from just before the chorus through the first half of the chorus, so that was where I struggled most, and probably the part I was most particular about.

Where do you feel you managed to “surpass” it?

If anything, I think it has a different kind of appeal. The demo had its own appeal as a demo, that relaxed quality of casually singing at home. The released version has more feeling put into it, I guess… it really comes across as emotional. Personally, I really feel that difference.

When you shared the demo, you posted on TikTok that you were “still deciding on a title.”

I read through lots of comments and ideas. A lot of people said they “want to use it at their wedding,” so when I was finishing the full version, I thought, “Maybe I could make it a bit more like a wedding song.” It was a great reference and really helped me out.

Tell us about the changes in your environment, too. BMSG, the company you partnered with, brings together artists brimming with individuality under the slogan “​​Never extinguish talent.” What kind of inspiration and influence have you gotten in that environment?

At first​,​ I was completely wrapped up in my own stuff, but I recently took part in BMSG FES for the first time and got to see the other BMSG artists perform, and I was really inspired. Personally, it was my first time riding a trolley float, so that was a great experience too. [Laughs] Also, it reminded me that it truly is a music-first company, and since what everyone expresses is pretty different, I thought it was really great that every group and every artist puts their own colors front and center without being bound by any set format.

BMSG also has a vision called “TYOISM,” about reaching the world from Tokyo. With you joining — someone who moved to the U.S. at 14 and is still based there and in Japan — it seems likely to spark some new chemistry.

I think I have a pretty good feel for how things are done abroad, so when it comes to opening doors to global challenges, I definitely want to do that together with BMSG. Mr. Hidaka (SKY-HI) and I had talked about that kind of thing from the start.

Now that five years have passed since your 2021 major-label debut, how do you see the phase you’re in as an artist?

Right now might be a phase where I just make music really freely, without overthinking it. Last year or so, I was thinking a lot about things like, “I want to release this kind of song and become this kind of artist,” but lately I feel like I’ve thought it all through, so it’s more like I’m going with the flow.

What artists have you been listening to a lot lately?

I’ve had Remi Wolf’s album (Mud) on heavy rotation lately. I really love indie rock-type stuff, too.

You have a headlining show set at Nippon Budokan in January 2027. Tell us how you’re feeling going in.

The Budokan is truly a stage I’ve set as a goal, so I think it’s going to be a really flashy show. [Laughs] More people than ever are listening to my music, and I’ve got a concert booked at my biggest venue yet, so I’m super happy and feel like all I have to do is keep doing what I’ve been doing. I want to keep creating spaces where everyone can come together as one and enjoy music at bigger and bigger venues.

—This interview by Takuto Ueda first appeared on Billboard Japan

How open source is driving positive change.

Organizations worldwide are incorporating open source methodologies into the way they build and ship their own software.

Helpful tips on securing web applications.

Learn about core challenges in DevSecOps, and how you can start addressing them with AI and automation.

In the Codename MDASH pilot, GitHub and Microsoft turned thousands of raw AI findings into 99 checked, private reports with suggested fixes for six widely used open source projects. Maintainers have already fixed more than half.

AI has made finding potential vulnerabilities fast and cheap. Fixing them still takes human judgment, and in open source, that work falls to maintainers. Many are already flooded with AI-generated security findings, including duplicates and reports that aren’t vulnerabilities at all. Another raw scan doesn’t help them. Anyone pointing a new model at a project should ask its maintainers first and come with fixes, not just findings.

Maintainers are under intense pressure from this flood of reports. As Christian Grobmeier, a maintainer of Log4j, has put it, the AI defending open source has to be better than the AI attacking it.

AI shouldn’t add to maintainers’ workload. So, with maintainers, GitHub and Microsoft ran a pilot together: could AI help maintainers ship fixes instead of handing them more findings?

GitHub and Microsoft are using Codename MDASH, Microsoft’s multi-model agentic scanning harness, to find and fix security vulnerabilities in widely used open source software, strengthening the foundation that much of the world’s code is built on.

Our pilot had two goals: send only findings that matter, and never send a maintainer an unverified finding. Here’s how it worked:

Reports and fixes stayed on GitHub, where maintainers already work.

The result: Codename MDASH, using Microsoft’s MAI Cyber model, flagged thousands of possible issues across the six projects. After AI triage and hand review by seven Microsoft FORGE Lab researchers, maintainers received 99 private reports. As of November 15, they had fixed 55, and 14 of those fixes were already in a release.

Six projects took part, all alumni of the GitHub Secure Open Source Fund. Their maintainers agreed to the scan, received every report privately, decided what to fix, and shared what worked:

These lessons come from what the six projects fixed and what their maintainers chose to share. Six projects is a small sample, so read them as early lessons.

Fixing is where maintainers spend their time. So every report arrived already checked, with a suggested fix to review, adapt, and merge. Maintainers started from a well-scoped patch, not a raw finding.

The first fixes shipped about a week after the initial scan. Caddy’s maintainers fixed all 22 of the reports they received in a week or less. Maintainers used the suggested fixes, some as written and some with changes.

AutoGPT’s maintainers had recently shipped a new check, and it was broken. The report landed three days later and pointed at the right line. They took the fix further than the suggested patch, wrote it within a day, and had it in production less than a week later.

Finding vulnerabilities is no longer our bottleneck. Fixing them is the hard part, and that depends on maintainers.

Maintainers got a short list, not a flood. Express maintainers said the reports cut through the noise. Caddy maintainers said they surfaced obscure bugs and filled gaps that earlier reports had missed. For FastMCP, the scan went beyond what AI scanners had given them before. ImageMagick, maintained by a two-person team, received 22 reports.

AI did the finding, researchers did the checking, and maintainers stayed in charge.

One ImageMagick report looked like a kind of issue the maintainers had seen before. Its summary showed it wasn’t and pointed to the exact lines of code. They were able to push a fix in 15 minutes.

Normally, a security advisory would take us around 30 minutes to 1 hour on average to read, understand, and make a fix. It is a huge difference.

Codename MDASH was able to find unknown, obscure security bugs in our project with succinct reports, and it filled in gaps from some prior reports that hadn’t been noticed.

The pilot worked because people did careful work at every step, and that work doesn’t scale yet. Seven researchers spent about two days per project checking findings by hand. Meanwhile, low-quality reports still reach maintainers every day.

For open source, fixes need to be delivered via private vulnerability reporting (PVR). Maintainers need to run CI/CD tests on private forks to verify those fixes. And to close the gap between defenders and attackers, maintainers need access to cyber-capable models to verify a proof of concept, before and after the fix.

The Codename MDASH scan provided unique insights into the security of our project above and beyond what AI scanners have provided us in the past

If you’re a maintainer, this approach changes what a security report costs you. A checked, private report that comes with a suggested fix takes less of your time. You shouldn’t have to triage a scanner’s output. That’s the sender’s job.

If you use AI to find vulnerabilities in open source, maintainers’ feedback points to this standard:

Features are easier and easier to ship, but in the future maintaining software will probably come down to who cares enough to manage the security.

Codename MDASH is very useful for cutting through the noise of vulnerability reports.

Everyone downstream benefits from this system. When a maintainer publishes a security advisory on GitHub and it’s reviewed for the GitHub Advisory Database, Dependabot can alert the repositories that depend on that package. That’s the idea behind the GitHub Secure Open Source Fund: 216 projects so far, with more than 300 maintainers fixing more than 4,000 vulnerabilities in software downloaded more than 20 billion times a month.

To the maintainers of AutoGPT, Caddy, Express, FastMCP, ImageMagick, and Netty: thank you for your time, your trust, and your feedback. Thank you also to the Codename MDASH team at Microsoft Security, the MAI Cyber team, the researchers of Microsoft FORGE Lab, and GitHub Security Lab.

Maintainers do the fixing. Our job is to make sure they never do it alone.

Hear from maintainers about their experience with Codename MDASH. 👇

All six projects in this pilot are alumni of the GitHub Secure Open Source Fund, which gives each project $10,000 through GitHub Sponsors, plus security training and a supportive alumni community.

The GitHub Secure Open Source Fund, powered by GitHub Sponsors, funds open source projects that underpin the digital supply chain, catalyze innovation, and are critical to the AI stack. Want your company to help secure the open source it depends on? Become a funding partner through the Fund page. Learn more about the Fund or apply.

Through the Fund, maintainers receive security training and Azure credits from Microsoft for Startups. That support sits alongside Microsoft’s European Security Program and its Secure Future Initiative, whose principle is security above all else. Most organizations are downstream in the same way. If your software depends on open source, do the same: back the maintainers you rely on.

Did FBI plant the seeds for another spinoff?

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Warning! Spoilers ahead for FBI Season 9, Episode 7, “Posterity,” streaming now with a Paramount+ subscription.

With FBI now in its ninth season on the 2026 TV schedule, the stakes have certainly been rising, and I’m not just talking about the professional ones. A show like FBI not only digs into the work aspect of the characters but the personal lives as well, even if it’s not shown as much. This week’s episode had a particularly surprising personal update for one of the characters, and that made me realize I had to ask the stars about spinoff potential.

Shantel VanSanten returned to FBI as Special Agent Nina Chase in Monday’s episode, “Posterity,” which marked her second appearance on the series since FBI: Most Wanted’s cancellation in 2025. Her return came just a couple of weeks after it was revealed that Nina had been offered a job to lead her own task force in San Diego, leading Scola (John Boyd) to question the logistics of their relationship and raising their son, Dougie. As Nina continued to pack and get in one last case at 26 Fed, Scola was still wrestling with how they were going to make things work. Ultimately, he told Nina he was going to put in a transfer and follow her to San Diego, meaning another FBI exit could be looming.

As Scola pointed out, though, it will still probably be a while until the transfer goes through. However, with him eventually moving out to California and Scolina taking the FBI by storm down there, this has spinoff potential written all over it. It would be fun to follow Scola and Nina in San Diego as Nina led her own task force, but would it ever happen? VanSanten and Boyd gave their take to CinemaBlend and, hilariously, have their own opinions on the ordeal:

FBI has been growing as a franchise, like any Dick Wolf series, so it’s totally logical to wonder about a spinoff coming from this update. Having two fan-favorite characters moving across the country for the job would certainly be a missed opportunity for another series, in my humble opinion. While it sounds like there aren’t any plans, and Boyd seems to be sticking around on FBI for the time being, if VanSanten has her way, we very well could be getting a Scolina spinoff at some point in the future. While we wait and hope for that, I'm just glad that it seems like Nina is finally getting closure.

As for what could happen in a potential Scolina spinoff, well, the possibilities are endless. Nina leading her own task force and seeing how she evolves in that leadership position would definitely be a great focus point. Scola would probably be settling into a new FBI field office, but the two of them would still stick to their deal of not going on cases together. We’d also probably get more of them and Dougie, who we definitely have not seen enough of. The story is there, and so are the characters, and I would love it if it happened. But if not, I’d hope that Boyd isn’t leaving FBI any time soon.

Now, for the time being, Scola will continue working out of 26 Fed. Considering all the cast changes that FBI has been through in recent years, I’m hoping that Boyd doesn’t leave the series any time soon, no matter how much I want a Scolina spinoff. Nina may be moving, but fans can still see Scola and co. in new episodes of FBI on Mondays at 8 p.m. ET on CBS, streaming the next day on Paramount+.

America's hyperscale bit barns draw as much power as entire states

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Harvard study claims emissions attributable to datacenters are two to three times higher than in 2018

Just 403 hyperscale datacenters consumed an estimated 1.8 percent of US electricity in 2024-25, and their carbon dioxide emissions were roughly two to three times an earlier forecast for 2018.

Research led by the Harvard T.H. Chan School of Public Health and the Harvard Data Science Initiative looked specifically at hyperscale facilities, the largest class of server farms that typically support cloud computing and AI processing.

The published study, "Assessing the carbon emissions and energy consumption of US hyperscale data centres," estimates their combined electricity use approached that of a single state such as Indiana, Michigan, or Tennessee under the highest-load scenario.

The researchers identified 403 hyperscale facilities by using a larger datacenter dataset, satellite imagery, and building footprints on OpenStreetMap.

Rather than reading electricity meters, the researchers worked out consumption from each site's electrical capacity, the hours in a year, and assumptions about how heavily the facility was running.

Across four operating scenarios, ranging from low-intensity use to AI-heavy workloads, electricity draw was believed to total 68-99 terawatt-hours (TWh). Each of those states consumed approximately 100 TWh during the study period.

The researchers also calculated carbon dioxide (CO2) emissions using eGRID2023 plant-level generation and emissions data from the US Environmental Protection Agency (EPA). Emissions ranged from 21 million to 31 million metric tons of CO2 across the scenarios, with a central estimate of 25.7 million. That compares with 10.5 million for 2018.

The study covered May 2024 to April 2025, so electricity demand and carbon emissions are likely to be even higher now, the researchers say.

"What we're seeing is already striking, but it's only a snapshot of a rapidly expanding industry," said Gianluca Guidi, lead author on the paper and postdoctoral fellow in the Department of Biostatistics at Harvard.

"The datacenter boom hasn't slowed down since our study period ended in April 2025. New facilities have been built, existing campuses have expanded, and demand for AI computing continues to grow. That means the electricity use and carbon footprint we're reporting could already be substantially larger now in 2026."

Harvard's report also considers the mix of fuels being used to generate the electricity that powers all those hyperscale data houses. The mix varies considerably between regional grids, so where a hyperscale facility is built makes a substantial difference to its attributed emissions.

Across the sites studied, roughly 54 percent of the electricity attributed to their consumption came from fossil fuels, mainly natural gas and coal. The electricity attributed to these facilities nevertheless had a lower average carbon intensity than the US grid overall.

The researchers acknowledge limitations, including that there wasn't enough available data to study all hyperscale sites in the US, and that the data did not allow them to differentiate between power used for general-purpose compute versus AI-specialized operations at individual campuses. ®
